\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\prime}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) \right] \stackrel {(i)} {=} \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {\infty} \left[ \lambda_ {k} \left(\sigma^ {\prime}\right) \right] ^ {2} [ b (d, k) ] ^ {2} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ q _ {k} ^ {(d)} (\sqrt {d} \langle \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) q _ {k} ^ {(d)} (\sqrt {d} \langle \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\prime}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) \right] \\ \stackrel {(i i)} {=} \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {\infty} \left[ \lambda_ {k} (\sigma^ {\prime}) \right] ^ {2} b (d, k) q _ {k} ^ {(d)} (\langle \pmb {x}, \pmb {x} ^ {\prime} \rangle). \\ \end{array}
$$

here,  $(i)$  is due to the orthogonality of  $q_{k}^{(d)}(\sqrt{d}\langle \pmb{x},\cdot \rangle)$  and  $q_{k^{\prime}}^{(d)}(\sqrt{d}\langle \pmb{x}^{\prime},\cdot \rangle)$  for  $k\neq k^{\prime}$  as well as lemma 24 (exchanging summation with expectation), and  $(ii)$  is due to the property (40).

thus, we obtain

$$
k (\pmb {x}, \pmb {x} ^ {\prime}) = \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {\infty} \left[ \lambda_ {k} (\sigma^ {\prime}) \right] ^ {2} b (d, k) q _ {k} ^ {(d)} (\langle \pmb {x}, \pmb {x} ^ {\prime} \rangle) \frac {\langle \pmb {x} , \pmb {x} ^ {\prime} \rangle}{d}.
$$

we use the recurrence formula to simplify the above expression.

$$
\begin{array}{l} k (\pmb {x}, \pmb {x} ^ {\prime}) = \sum_ {k \geq 1} \frac {k}{2 k + d - 2} \left[ \lambda_ {k} (\sigma^ {\prime}) \right] ^ {2} b (d, k) q _ {k - 1} ^ {(d)} (\langle \pmb {x}, \pmb {x} ^ {\prime} \rangle) + \sum_ {k \geq 0} \frac {k + d - 2}{2 k + d - 2} \left[ \lambda_ {k} (\sigma^ {\prime}) \right] ^ {2} b (d, k) q _ {k + 1} ^ {(d)} (\langle \pmb {x}, \pmb {x} ^ {\prime} \rangle) \\ = \sum_ {k \geq 0} \frac {k + 1}{2 k + d} \left[ \lambda_ {k + 1} \left(\sigma^ {\prime}\right) \right] ^ {2} b (d, k + 1) q _ {k} ^ {(d)} \left(\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {j} \rangle\right) + \sum_ {k \geq 1} \frac {k + d - 3}{2 k + d - 4} \left[ \lambda_ {k - 1} \left(\sigma^ {\prime}\right) \right] ^ {2} b (d, k - 1) q _ {k} ^ {(k)} \left(\langle \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\prime} \rangle\right) \\ = \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {\infty} \gamma_ {k} q _ {k} ^ {(d)} \left(\langle \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\prime} \rangle\right). \tag {49} \\ \end{array}
$$

we thus proved that eq. (47) holds pointwise for every  $\pmb{x},\pmb{x}^{\prime}$ . calling  $k^{\ell}$  the sum of the first  $\ell$  terms on the right hand side, we also obtain  $\| k - k^{\ell}\|_{l^{2}(\mathbb{s}^{d - 1}(\sqrt{d})\times \mathbb{s}^{d - 1}(\sqrt{d}))}\to 0$  by an application of fatou's lemma. finally the last norm coincides with hilbert-schmidt norm, when we view these kernels as operators, and hence implies convergence in operator norm.

the asymptotic formulas for  $\gamma_{k}$  follow by the convergence of gegenbauer expansion to hermite expansion discussed in section 6.

lemma 2 (structure of infinite-width kernel matrix). assume  $(\pmb{x}_i)_{i\leq n}\sim_{iid}\mathrm{unif}(\mathbb{s}^{d - 1}(\sqrt{d}))$ , and the activation function to satisfy assumption 3.2. then we can decompose  $\mathbf{k}$  as follows

$$
\mathbf {k} = \gamma_ {> \ell} \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {\delta} \tag {50}
$$

where  $\gamma_{> \ell} := \sum_{k > \ell} \gamma_k = \sum_{k \geq \ell} \mu_k^2 + o_d(1)$  and

$$
\lambda_ {\leq \ell} ^ {2} := \operatorname {d i a g} \big \{\gamma_ {0}, \underbrace {b (d , 1) ^ {- 1} \gamma_ {1} , \ldots , b (d , 1) ^ {- 1} \gamma_ {1}} _ {b (d, 1)}, \ldots , \underbrace {b (d , \ell) ^ {- 1} \gamma_ {\ell} , \ldots , b (d , \ell) ^ {- 1} \gamma_ {\ell}} _ {b (d, \ell)} \big \}
$$

here,  $\gamma_{k}\geq 0$  and  $\gamma_{k} = \mu_{k - 1}^{2} + o_{d}(1)$  (we denote  $\mu_{-1} = 0$ ).

further there exists a constant  $c$  such that, for  $n(\log n)^{c} \leq d^{\ell + 1}$ , the remainder term  $\delta$  satisfies, with very high probability,

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \sqrt {\frac {n (\log n) ^ {c}}{d ^ {\ell + 1}}}.
$$

finally, if  $n \geq c d^{\ell} (\log d)^{2}$ , with very high probability,

$$
\left\| n ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} - \mathbf {i} _ {n} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq c \sqrt {\frac {d ^ {\ell} (\log d) ^ {2}}{n}}. \tag {51}
$$

in the case  $\ell = 1$ , the above upper bound can be replaced by  $\sqrt{cd / n}$ .

proof of lemma 2. using lemma 1, and using property (41) to express

$$
q _ {k} ^ {(k)} \left(\left\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {j} \right\rangle\right) = \left[ b (d, k) \right] ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {= \ell} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}) \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {= \ell} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {j}) ^ {\top}
$$

for  $k\leq \ell$  , we obtain the decomposition (50), where

$$
\boldsymbol {\delta} := \sum_ {k = \ell + 1} ^ {\infty} \gamma_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {q} _ {k} - \mathbf {i} _ {n}\right),
$$

and  $q_{k} \coloneqq (q_{k}^{(d)}(\langle \pmb{x}_{i}, \pmb{x}_{j} \rangle))_{i,j \leq n}$ . note that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \gamma_ {> \ell} = \sum_ {k \geq \ell + 2} \frac {k}{2 k + d - 2} b (d, k) \left[ \lambda_ {k} \left(\sigma^ {\prime}\right) \right] ^ {2} + \sum_ {k \geq \ell} \frac {k + d - 2}{2 k + d - 2} b (d, k) \left[ \lambda_ {k} \left(\sigma^ {\prime}\right) \right] ^ {2} \\ = \sum_ {\ell \leq k \leq \ell + 1} \frac {k + d - 2}{2 k + d - 2} b (d, k) \left[ \lambda_ {k} \left(\sigma^ {\prime}\right) \right] ^ {2} + \sum_ {k \geq \ell + 2} b (d, k) \left[ \lambda_ {k} \left(\sigma^ {\prime}\right) \right] ^ {2} \\ = \sum_ {k \geq \ell} \mu_ {k} ^ {2} + o _ {d} (1). \\ \end{array}
$$

the last equality involves interchanging the limit  $\lim_{d\to \infty}$  with the summation. we explain the validity as follows. for any integer  $m > \ell +2$

$$
\bigl | \sum_ {k \geq \ell + 2} b (d, k) [ \lambda_ {k} (\sigma^ {\prime}) ] ^ {2} - \sum_ {k \geq \ell} \mu_ {k} ^ {2} \bigr | \leq \sum_ {k \geq \ell + 2} ^ {m} \bigl | b (d, k) [ \lambda_ {k} (\sigma^ {\prime}) ] ^ {2} - \mu_ {k} ^ {2} \bigr | + \sum_ {k > m} b (d, k) [ \lambda_ {k} (\sigma^ {\prime}) ] ^ {2} + \sum_ {k > m} \mu_ {k} ^ {2}.
$$

by first taking  $d\to \infty$  and then  $m\rightarrow \infty$  , we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \limsup_{d\to \infty}\big|\sum_{k\geq \ell +2}b(d,k)[\lambda_{k}(\sigma^{\prime})]^{2} - \sum_{k\geq \ell}\mu_{k}^{2}\big|\leq \lim_{m\to \infty}\limsup_{d\to \infty}\sum_{k > m}b(d,k)[\lambda_{k}(\sigma^{\prime})]^{2}, \\ = \limsup_{d\to \infty}\| \sigma^{\prime}\|_{l^{2}(\tau_{d - 1}^{1})}^{2} - \lim_{m\to \infty}\liminf_{d\to \infty}\sum_{k\leq m}b(d,k)[\lambda_{k}(\sigma^{\prime})]^{2} \\ = \| \sigma^ {\prime} \| _ {l ^ {2} (\rho)} ^ {2} - \lim  _ {m \rightarrow \infty} \sum_ {k \leq m} \mu_ {k} (\sigma^ {\prime}) ^ {2} = 0. \\ \end{array}
$$

here in the last line we recall that  $\rho$  is the standard gaussian measure, and we used dominated convergence to show that  $\| \sigma^{\prime}\|_{l^{2}(\widetilde{\tau}_{d - 1}^{1})}^{2}\to \| \sigma^{\prime}\|_{l^{2}(\rho)}^{2}$ . by proposition e.1, we have with very high probability,

$$
\sup _ {k > \ell} \left\| \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} - \mathbf {i} _ {n} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \sqrt {\frac {n (\log n) ^ {c}}{d ^ {\ell + 1}}}.
$$

thus,  $\pmb{\delta} = \sum_{k > \ell}\gamma_{\ell}(\pmb{q}_k - \mathbf{i}_n)$  satisfies

$$
\| \pmb {\delta} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \gamma_ {> \ell} \sup _ {k > \ell} \left\| \pmb {q} _ {k} - \mathbf {i} _ {d} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \sqrt {\frac {n (\log n) ^ {c}}{d ^ {\ell + 1}}}.
$$

finally, denote  $d = \sum_{k\leq \ell}b(d,k)$ . the matrix  $\psi_{\leq \ell}\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times d}$  has  $n$  i.i.d. rows, denote them by  $\pmb {\psi}(\pmb {x}_i)$ ,  $i\leq n$ , where

$$
\boldsymbol {\psi} (\boldsymbol {x}) := \left(y _ {k, t} (\boldsymbol {x})\right) _ {t, \leq b (d, k), k \leq \ell}. \tag {52}
$$

by orthonormality of the spherical harmonics (42), the covariance of these vectors is  $\mathbb{e}\{\pmb {\psi}(\pmb {x})\pmb {\psi}(\pmb {x})^{\top}\} =$ $\mathbf{i}_d$  . further, for any  $\pmb {x}\in \mathbb{s}^{d - 1}(\sqrt{d})$  , we have

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\psi} (\boldsymbol {x}) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {\ell} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {b (d, k)} y _ {k, t} (\boldsymbol {x}) ^ {2} = \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {\ell} b (d, k) = d. \tag {53}
$$

by [ver18][theorem 5.6.1 and exercise 5.6.4], we obtain

$$
\left\| n ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} - \mathbf {i} _ {n} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq c \left(\sqrt {\frac {d (\log d + u)}{n}} + \frac {d (\log d + u)}{n}\right), \tag {54}
$$

with probability at least  $1 - 2e^{-u}$ . the claim follows by setting  $u = (\log d)^2$ , and noting that  $d \leq c'd^\ell$ .

# 7.2 concentration of neural tangent kernel: proof of theorem 3.2

let  $c_0 > 0$  be a sufficiently large constant (which is determined later). define the truncated function

$$
\varphi (x) = \sigma^ {\prime} (x) \mathbf {1} \{| x | \leq c _ {0} \log (n n d) \}.
$$

for  $k \in [n]$ , we also define matrices  $\mathbf{d}_k, \mathbf{h}_k \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times n}$ , and truncated kernels  $\mathbf{k}^0$  and  $\mathbf{k}_n^0$  as

$$
\mathbf {d} _ {k} = \operatorname {d i a g} \left\{\varphi \left(\left\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {w} _ {k} \right\rangle\right), \dots , \varphi \left(\left\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {n}, \boldsymbol {w} _ {k} \right\rangle\right) \right\}, \quad \mathbf {k} ^ {0} = \frac {1}{d} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ \mathbf {d} _ {k} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \mathbf {d} _ {k} \right] \tag {55}
$$

$$
\mathbf {h} _ {k} = \frac {1}{d} (\mathbf {k} ^ {0}) ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {d} _ {k} \mathbf {x} \mathbf {x} ^ {\top} \mathbf {d} _ {k} (\mathbf {k} ^ {0}) ^ {- 1 / 2}, \qquad \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {0} = \frac {1}{n d} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {n} \mathbf {d} _ {k} \mathbf {x} \mathbf {x} ^ {\top} \mathbf {d} _ {k}. \tag {56}
$$

note that by definition and the assumption on the activation function, namely assumption 3.2, we have  $|\varphi(x)| \leq b(1 + (c_0 \log(nnd))^b)$ .

note that  $\mathcal{a}_{\gamma}$  is a very high probability event as a consequence of lemma 2. we shall treat  $\pmb{x}_1, \dots, \pmb{x}_n$  as deterministic vectors such that the conditions in the event  $\mathcal{a}_{\gamma}$  hold.

step 1: the effect of truncation is small. first, we realize that

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \big (\mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {0} \neq \mathbf {k} _ {n} \big) \leq \mathbb {p} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \big (\max  _ {i, k} | \langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {w} _ {k} \rangle | > c _ {0} \log (n n d) \big) \leq n n \mathbb {p} \big (| \langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {w} _ {1} \rangle | > c _ {0} \log (n n d) \big).
$$

by the fact that uniform spherical random vector is subgaussian [ver10][sect. 5.2.5], we pick  $c_0$  sufficiently large such that

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left(\left| \langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {w} _ {1} \rangle \right| > c _ {0} \log (n n d)\right) \leq c \exp \left(- c (\log (n n d)) ^ {2}\right). \tag {57}
$$

thus, with very high probability,  $\mathbf{k}_n^0 = \mathbf{k}_n$ .

furthermore, for  $i,j\in [n]$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} (\mathbf {k} - \mathbf {k} ^ {0}) _ {i j} = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \big [ \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {j}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) (1 - \mathbf {1} _ {a _ {i}}) \mathbf {1} _ {a _ {j}} \big ] \frac {\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} , \boldsymbol {x} _ {j} \rangle}{d} \\ + \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \big [ \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {j}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) (1 - \mathbf {1} _ {a _ {j}}) \big ] \frac {\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} , \boldsymbol {x} _ {j} \rangle}{d} =: i _ {1} + i _ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $a_{i}\coloneqq \{\left|\langle \pmb{x}_{i},\pmb{w}\rangle \right|\leq c_{0}\log (nnd)\}$ . for the first term, we derive

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| i _ {1} \right| \stackrel {(i)} {\leq} \left| \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \big [ \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {j}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) (1 - \mathbf {1} _ {a _ {i}}) \mathbf {1} _ {a _ {j}} \big ] \right| \\ \stackrel {(i i)} {\leq} \left\{\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ \left(\sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle)\right) ^ {4} \right] \right\} ^ {1 / 4} \cdot \left\{\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ \left(\sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {j}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle)\right) ^ {4} \right] \right\} ^ {1 / 4} \cdot \left\{\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ (1 - \mathbf {1} _ {a _ {i}}) ^ {2} \right] \right\} ^ {1 / 2} \\ \stackrel {(i i i)} {\leq} c (d ^ {b} + 1) \left\{\mathbb {p} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left(\left| \langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle \right| > c _ {0} \log (n n d)\right) \right\} ^ {1 / 2} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $(i)$  is due to  $|\langle \pmb{x}_i,\pmb{x}_j\rangle |\leq \| \pmb{x}_i\| \cdot \| \pmb{x}_j\| = d$ $(ii)$  is due to hölder's inequality, and  $(iii)$  follows from the polynomial growth assumption on  $\sigma^{\prime}$ . by (57), we can choose  $c_0$  large enough such that  $|i_{1}|\leq c(nnd)^{-2}$ . similarly, we can prove that  $|i_{2}|\leq c(nnd)^{-2}$ . thus,

$$
\left\| \mathbf {k} ^ {0} - \mathbf {k} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq n \max  _ {i j} \left| (\mathbf {k} ^ {0} - \mathbf {k}) _ {i j} \right| \leq \frac {c}{n n d}. \tag {58}
$$

since we work on the event  $\mathbf{k} \succeq \gamma \mathbf{i}_n$ , this implies

$$
\left\| \mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2} \left(\mathbf {k} ^ {0} - \mathbf {k}\right) \mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {c}{\gamma n n d}. \tag {59}
$$

step 2: concentration of truncated kernel. next, we will use matrix concentration inequality [ver18][thm. 5.4.1] for  $\sum_{k=1}^{n} \mathbf{h}_k$ . first, we observe that (58) implies that  $\mathbf{k}^0 \succeq (\gamma - o_d(1)) \cdot \mathbf{i}_n$ . together with the bound on  $\|x\|_{\mathrm{op}}$  and the deterministic bound on  $\|\mathbf{d}_k\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ , we have a deterministic bound on  $\|\mathbf{h}_k\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ .

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \mathbf {h} _ {k} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {1}{d \lambda_ {\mathrm {m i n}} (\mathbf {k} ^ {0})} \| \mathbf {d} _ {k} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \cdot \| \boldsymbol {x} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \\ \leq \frac {1}{d (\gamma - o _ {d} (1))} \left[ b \left(1 + \left(c _ {0} \log n n d\right) ^ {b}\right) \right] ^ {2} \cdot 4 (\sqrt {n} + \sqrt {d}) ^ {2} \\ \leq \frac {c (n + d)}{d} \big (\log (n n d) \big) ^ {c}. \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $c$  is a sufficiently large constant. this also implies  $\| \mathbf{h}_k - \mathbb{e}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[\mathbf{h}_k]\|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq (n + d)\log (nnd)^c /d$ . we will make use of the following simple fact: if  $\mathbf{a}_1, \mathbf{a}_2$  are p.s.d. that satisfy  $\mathbf{a}_1 \preceq \mathbf{a}_2$ , then  $q\mathbf{a}_1\mathbf{q}^\top \preceq q\mathbf{a}_2\mathbf{q}^\top$ . we use this on  $\mathbf{h}_k^2$  and find

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {h} _ {k} ^ {2} = \frac {1}{d ^ {2}} (\mathbf {k} ^ {0}) ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {d} _ {k} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \mathbf {d} _ {k} (\mathbf {k} ^ {0}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {d} _ {k} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \mathbf {d} _ {k} (\mathbf {k} ^ {0}) ^ {- 1 / 2} \\ \preceq \frac {1}{d ^ {2}} (\mathbf {k} ^ {0}) ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {d} _ {k} \mathbf {x} \mathbf {x} ^ {\top} \mathbf {x} \mathbf {x} ^ {\top} \mathbf {d} _ {k} (\mathbf {k} ^ {0}) ^ {- 1 / 2} \cdot (\gamma - o _ {d} (1)) ^ {- 1} \big [ b (1 + (c _ {0} \log (n n d)) ^ {b}) \big ] ^ {2} \\ \preceq \frac {1}{d ^ {2}} (\mathbf {k} ^ {0}) ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {d} _ {k} \mathbf {x} \mathbf {x} ^ {\top} \mathbf {d} _ {k} (\mathbf {k} ^ {0}) ^ {- 1 / 2} \cdot (\gamma - o _ {d} (1)) ^ {- 1} \big [ b (1 + (c _ {0} \log (n n d)) ^ {b}) \big ] ^ {2} \cdot 4 (\sqrt {n} + \sqrt {d}) ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

taking the expectation  $\mathbb{e}_{\boldsymbol{w}}$ , we get

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ \mathbf {h} _ {k} ^ {2} \right] \preceq \frac {1}{d} (\gamma - o _ {d} (1)) ^ {- 1} \left[ b (1 + \left(c _ {0} \log (n n d)\right) ^ {b}) \right] ^ {2} \cdot 4 (\sqrt {n} + \sqrt {d}) ^ {2} \cdot \mathbf {i} _ {n} \preceq \frac {c (n + d)}{d} \left(\log (n n d)\right) ^ {c} \cdot \mathbf {i} _ {n}.
$$

this implies

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \big (\mathbf {h} _ {k} - \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} [ \mathbf {h} _ {k} ] \big) ^ {2} = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} [ \mathbf {h} _ {k} ^ {2} ] - \big (\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} [ \mathbf {h} _ {k} ] \big) ^ {2} \preceq \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} [ \mathbf {h} _ {k} ^ {2} ] \preceq o \big (\frac {n + d}{d} \mathrm {p o l y l o g} (n n d) \big) \cdot \mathbf {i} _ {n}.
$$

now we apply the matrix bernstein's inequality [ver18][thm. 5.4.1] to the matrix sum  $\sum_{k=1}^{n} \mathbf{h}_k - \mathbb{e}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[\mathbf{h}_k]$ . we obtain

$$
\mathbb {p} \left(\left| \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {n} \mathbf {h} _ {k} - \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} [ \mathbf {h} _ {k} ] \right| \geq t\right) \leq 2 n \cdot \exp \left(- \frac {t ^ {2} / 2}{v + l t / 3}\right), \tag {60}
$$

where

$$
l = \frac {n + d}{d} \big (\log (n n d) \big) ^ {c}, \qquad v = \frac {n (n + d)}{d} \big (\log (n n d) \big) ^ {c}.
$$

we choose a sufficient large constant  $c^\prime$  and set

$$
t = \max \left\{\sqrt {\frac {n (n + d) (\log (n n d)) ^ {c ^ {\prime}}}{d}}, \frac {(n + d) (\log (n n d)) ^ {c ^ {\prime}}}{d} \right\}
$$

so that the right-hand side of (60) is no larger than  $2n\cdot \exp (-(\log (nnd)^2))$ . this proves that with very high probability,

$$
\left\| \left(\mathbf {k} ^ {0}\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {0} \left(\mathbf {k} ^ {0}\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} - \mathbf {i} _ {n} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \sqrt {\frac {(n + d) (\log (n n d)) ^ {c ^ {\prime}}}{n d}} + \frac {(n + d) (\log (n n d)) ^ {c ^ {\prime}}}{n d}. \tag {61}
$$

step 3: back to original kernel. the inequality (59) implies that for large enough  $n$ ,  $\| (\mathbf{k}^0)^{1 / 2}\mathbf{k}^{-1 / 2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq c$ . therefore,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {0} - \mathbf {k}) \mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \\ \leq \left\| \mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {0} - \mathbf {k} ^ {0}) \mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} + \left\| \mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\mathbf {k} ^ {0} - \mathbf {k}) \mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \\ \leq \left\| \mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\mathbf {k} ^ {0}) ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \cdot \left\| (\mathbf {k} ^ {0}) ^ {- 1 / 2} (\mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {0} - \mathbf {k} ^ {0}) (\mathbf {k} ^ {0}) ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \cdot \left\| (\mathbf {k} ^ {0}) ^ {1 / 2} \mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} + \frac {c}{\gamma n n d} \\ \leq \sqrt {\frac {(n + d) (\log (n n d)) ^ {c ^ {\prime}}}{n d}} + \frac {(n + d) (\log (n n d)) ^ {c ^ {\prime}}}{n d} + \frac {c}{\gamma n n d}. \\ \end{array}
$$

since  $1 / (\gamma nnd) \leq o(1) \cdot c' \sqrt{(n + d)(\log(nnd))^{c'} / (nd)}$ , we can enlarge the constant  $c'$  appropriately to obtain that with very high probability,

$$
\left\| \mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {0} - \mathbf {k}) \mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \sqrt {\frac {(n + d) (\log (n n d)) ^ {c ^ {\prime}}}{n d}} + \frac {(n + d) (\log (n n d)) ^ {c ^ {\prime}}}{n d}.
$$

this completes the proof of theorem 3.2.

# 7.3 smallest eigenvalue of neural tangent kernel: proof of theorem 3.1

by lemma 2, we have, with high probability,

$$
\| \pmb {\delta} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \sqrt {\frac {n (\log n) ^ {c}}{d ^ {\ell + 1}}} \leq \sqrt {\frac {(\log n) ^ {c}}{(\log d) ^ {c _ {0}}}} \leq \sqrt {(\ell + 1) ^ {c} (\log d) ^ {c - c _ {0}}},
$$

where the second inequality is because by assumption 3.1,  $d^{\ell + 1} \geq n (\log d)^{c_0} \geq n$ , so  $\log n \leq (\ell + 1) \log d$ . we choose the constant  $c_0$  to be larger than  $c$ . so by weyl's inequality,

$$
\left| \lambda_ {\mathrm {m i n}} (\mathbf {k}) - \lambda_ {\mathrm {m i n}} (\gamma_ {> \ell} \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top}) \right| \leq \| \boldsymbol {\delta} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = o _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1).
$$

note that  $\psi_{\leq \ell}\lambda_{\leq \ell}^{2}\psi_{\leq \ell}^{\top}$  is always p.s.d., and it has rank at most  $d + 1$  if  $\ell = 1$  and  $o(d^{\ell})$  if  $\ell > 1$ . so

$$
\lambda_ {\min} (\gamma_ {> \ell} \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top}) \overset {(i)} {\geq} \gamma_ {> \ell} = v (\sigma) + o _ {d} (1) \quad \mathrm {a n d t h u s} \quad \lambda_ {\min} (\mathbf {k}) \overset {(i i)} {\geq} v (\sigma) - o _ {d} (1)
$$

and we can strengthen the inequalities  $\geq$  to equalities  $= \text{in} (i)$ ,  $(ii)$  if  $n > d + 1$ . by theorem 3.2 and eq. (14) in its following remark,

$$
(1 - o _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1)) \cdot \lambda_ {\min } (\mathbf {k}) \leq \lambda_ {\min } (\mathbf {k} _ {n}) \leq (1 + o _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1)) \cdot \lambda_ {\min } (\mathbf {k}).
$$

we conclude that  $\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(\mathbf{k}_n) \geq v(\sigma) - o_{d,\mathbb{p}}(1)$  and that the inequality can be replaced by an equality if  $n > d + 1$ .

# 8 generalization error: proof outline of theorem 3.3

in this section outline the proof our main result theorem 3.3, which characterizes the test error of nt regression. we describe the main scheme of proof, and how we treat the bias term. we refer to the appendix where the remaining steps (and most of the technical work) are carried out.

throughout, we will assume that the setting of that theorem (in particular, assumption 3.1 and 3.2) hold. we will further assume that eq. (10) in assumption 3.1 holds for the case  $\ell = 1$  as well. in section c we will refine our analysis to eliminate logarithmic factors for  $\ell = 1$ .

in nt regression the coefficients vector is given by eq. (16) and, more explicitly, eq. (17). the prediction function  $\widehat{f}_{\mathsf{nt}}(\pmb {x}) = \langle \pmb {\phi}(\pmb {x}),\widehat{\pmb{a}}\rangle$  can be written as

$$
\widehat {f} _ {\mathrm {n t}} (\boldsymbol {x}) = \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {y},
$$

where we denote  $\mathbf{k}_n(\cdot ,\pmb {x}) = (k_n(\pmb {x}_1,\pmb {x}),\dots ,k_n(\pmb {x}_n,\pmb {x}))^\top \in \mathbb{r}^n$ . define  $\pmb {f} = (f(\pmb {x}_1),\dots ,f(\pmb {x}_n))^\top$  and  $\varepsilon = (\varepsilon_{1},\ldots ,\varepsilon_{n})^{\top}$ . we now decompose the generalization error  $r_{\mathsf{nt}}(\lambda)$  into three errors.

$$
\begin{array}{l} r _ {\mathrm {n t}} (\lambda) = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \left(f (\boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {y}\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \left(f (\boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f}\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ + \varepsilon^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} \right] (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \varepsilon \\ - 2 \pmb {\varepsilon} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\pmb {x}} \big [ \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \pmb {x}) (f (\pmb {x}) - \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \pmb {x}) ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \pmb {f}) \big ] \\ =: e _ {\text {b i a s}} ^ {n} + e _ {\text {v a r}} ^ {n} - 2 e _ {\text {c r o s s}} ^ {n}. \\ \end{array}
$$

in the kernel ridge regression, the prediction function is

$$
\widehat {f} _ {\mathrm {k r r}} (\boldsymbol {x}) = \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {y}.
$$

similarly, we can decompose the associated generalization error  $r_{\mathsf{krr}}(\lambda)$  into three errors.

$$
\begin{array}{l} r _ {\mathsf {k r r}} (\lambda) = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \left(f (\boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {y}\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \left(f (\boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f}\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ + \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} \right] (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \\ - 2 \varepsilon^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) (f (\boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f}) \right] \\ =: e _ {\text {b i a s}} + e _ {\text {v a r}} - 2 e _ {\text {c r o s s}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

the first part of theorem 3.3, establishing that nt regression is well approximated by kernel ridge regression for overparametrized models, is an immediate consequence of the next statement.

theorem 8.1 (reduction to kernel ridge regression). there exists a constant  $c' > 0$  such that the following holds. if  $n \leq (\log(nd))^{c'}nd$ , then for any  $\lambda > 0$ , with high probability,

$$
\left| e _ {\text {b i a s}} ^ {n} - e _ {\text {b i a s}} \right| \leq \eta \| f \| _ {l ^ {2}} ^ {2}, \quad \left| e _ {\text {v a r}} ^ {n} - e _ {\text {v a r}} \right| \leq \eta \sigma_ {\varepsilon} ^ {2}, \tag {62}
$$

$$
\left| e _ {\text {c r o s s}} ^ {n} - e _ {\text {c r o s s}} \right| \leq \eta \| f \| _ {l ^ {2}} \sigma_ {\varepsilon}, \quad \text {w h e r e} \eta = \sqrt {\frac {n \left(c ^ {\prime} \log (n n d)\right) ^ {c ^ {\prime}}}{n d}}. \tag {63}
$$

as a consequence, we have  $r_{\mathsf{nt}}(\lambda) = r_{\mathsf{krr}}(\lambda) + o_{d,\mathbb{p}}\big((\| f\|_{l^2}^2 +\sigma_\varepsilon^2)\sqrt{\frac{n(\log(nnd))^{c'}}{nd}}\big)$ .

recall the decomposition of the infinite-width kernel into gegenbauer polynomials introduced in lemma 1. in section 3.4 we defined the polynomial kernel  $k^p(\boldsymbol{x}, \boldsymbol{x}')$  by truncating  $k(\boldsymbol{x}, \boldsymbol{x}')$  to the degree- $\ell$  polynomials. namely:

$$
k ^ {p} (\boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\prime}) = \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {\ell} \gamma_ {k} q _ {k} ^ {(d)} (\langle \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\prime} \rangle). \tag {64}
$$

we also define the matrix  $\mathbf{k}^p\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times n}$  and vector  $\mathbf{k}^p (\cdot ,\pmb {x})\in \mathbb{r}^n$  as in eq. (20). in polynomial ridge regression, the prediction function is

$$
\widehat {f} _ {\mathsf {p r r}} (\boldsymbol {x}) = \mathbf {k} ^ {p} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} ((\lambda + \gamma_ {> \ell}) \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} ^ {p}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {y}.
$$

its associated generalization error  $r_{\mathsf{prr}}(\lambda)$  into also decomposed into three errors.

$$
\begin{array}{l} r _ {\mathsf {p r r}} (\lambda) = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \left(f (\boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} ^ {p} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} ^ {p}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f}\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ + \varepsilon^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} ^ {p}) ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \mathbf {k} ^ {p} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) \mathbf {k} ^ {p} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} \right] (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} ^ {p}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \\ - 2 \varepsilon^ {\top} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} ^ {p}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \right. \mathbf {k} ^ {p} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) (f (\boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} ^ {p} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} ^ {p}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f}\left. \right)\left. \right] \\ =: e _ {\mathrm {b i a s}} ^ {p} (\lambda) + e _ {\mathrm {v a r}} ^ {p} (\lambda) - 2 e _ {\mathrm {c r o s s}} ^ {p} (\lambda). \\ \end{array}
$$

the second part of theorem 3.3 follows immediately from the following result.

theorem 8.2 (reduction to polynomial ridge regression). there exists a constant  $c' > 0$  such that the following holds. for any  $\lambda > 0$ , with high probability,

$$
\left| e _ {\text {b i a s}} (\lambda) - e _ {\text {b i a s}} ^ {p} (\lambda + \gamma_ {> \ell}) \right| \leq \eta^ {\prime} \| f \| _ {l ^ {2}} ^ {2}, \quad \left| e _ {\text {v a r}} (\lambda) - e _ {\text {v a r}} ^ {p} (\lambda + \gamma_ {> \ell}) \right| \leq \eta^ {\prime} \sigma_ {\varepsilon} ^ {2}, \tag {65}
$$

$$
\left| e _ {\text {c r o s s}} (\lambda) - e _ {\text {c r o s s}} ^ {p} (\lambda + \gamma_ {> \ell}) \right| \leq \eta^ {\prime} \| f \| _ {l ^ {2}} \sigma_ {\varepsilon}, \quad \text {w h e r e} \eta^ {\prime} := \sqrt {\frac {c ^ {\prime} (\log n) ^ {c ^ {\prime}} n}{d ^ {\ell + 1}}} \tag {66}
$$

as a consequence, we have  $r_{\mathsf{krr}}(\lambda) = r_{\mathsf{prr}}(\lambda + \gamma_{> \ell}) + o_{d,\mathbb{p}}\big((\| f\|_{l^2}^2 + \sigma_\varepsilon^2)\sqrt{\frac{c'(\log n)^{c'n}}{d^{\ell+1}}}\big)$ .

# 8.1 proof of theorem 8.1: bias term

in this section we prove the first inequality in eq. (62). since both sides are homogeneous in  $f$ , we will assume without loss of generality that  $\| f\|_{l^2} = 1$

first let us decompose  $e_{\mathrm{bias}}^{n}$ . define

$$
\mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) = \left(k (\boldsymbol {x} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {x}), \dots , k (\boldsymbol {x} _ {n}, \boldsymbol {x})\right) ^ {\top} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}, \qquad \mathbf {k} ^ {(2)} = \mathbb {e} \left[ \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} \right] \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n \times n},
$$

$$
\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) = \left(k _ {n} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {x}), \dots , k _ {n} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {n}, \boldsymbol {x})\right) ^ {\top} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}, \qquad \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {(2)} = \mathbb {e} \left[ \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} \right] \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n \times n}.
$$

then,

$$
e _ {\mathrm {b i a s}} ^ {n} = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \left(f (\boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f}\right) ^ {2} \right] = \| f \| _ {l ^ {2}} ^ {2} - 2 i _ {1} ^ {n} + i _ {2} ^ {n}
$$

where we define

$$
i _ {1} ^ {n} = \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) f (\boldsymbol {x}) \right],
$$

$$
i _ {2} ^ {n} = \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} \right] (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f}.
$$

we also decompose  $e_{\mathrm{bias}} = \| f\|_{l^2}^2 -2i_1 + i_2$ , where

$$
i _ {1} = \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} [ \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) f (\boldsymbol {x}) ],
$$

$$
i _ {2} = \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \big [ \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} \big ] (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f}.
$$

our goal is to show that  $i_1^n$  and  $i_1$  are close, and that  $i_2^n$  and  $i_2$  are close. let us pause here to explain the challenges and our proof strategy:

- first, our concentration result controls  $\| \mathbf{k}^{-1 / 2}(\mathbf{k}_n - \mathbf{k})\mathbf{k}^{-1 / 2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}$  but not  $\| \mathbf{k}^{-1}(\mathbf{k}_n - \mathbf{k})\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ , so it is crucial to balance the matrix differences as we do in the decomposition introduced below.  
- second, the relation between eigenvalues of  $\mathbf{k}_n$  and  $\mathbf{k}$  is not sufficient to control the generalization error (which is evident in the term  $\mathbf{k}_n^{-1}\mathbf{k}_n^{(2)}\mathbf{k}_n^{-1}$ ). we will therefore characterize the eigenvector structure as well.  
- third our bound of  $\| \mathbf{k}^{-1 / 2}(\mathbf{k}_n - \mathbf{k})\mathbf{k}^{-1 / 2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}$  does not allow us to control  $\mathbf{k}_n^{(2)} - \mathbf{k}^{(2)}$  from our previous analysis. we develop a new approach that exploits the independence of  $(\boldsymbol{w}_k)_{k\leq n}$ .

for the purpose of later use, we need a slightly general setup: let  $g \in l^2$  be a function and  $\pmb{h} \in \mathbb{r}^n$  a random vector. we begin the analysis by defining the following differences.

$$
\delta i _ {1 1} ^ {g, \pmb {h}} = \left[ \pmb {h} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} - \pmb {h} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} \right] \cdot \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\pmb {x}} \big [ \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \pmb {x}) g (\pmb {x}) \big ],
$$

$$
\delta i _ {1 2} ^ {g, \pmb {h}} = \pmb {h} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\pmb {x}} \left[ (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \pmb {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \pmb {x})) g (\pmb {x}) \right],
$$

$$
\delta i _ {2 1} ^ {\boldsymbol {h}} = \left[ \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} - \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} \right] \cdot \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {(2)} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \cdot \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h},
$$

$$
\delta i _ {2 2} ^ {\boldsymbol {h}} = \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} \cdot \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {(2)} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \cdot \left[ \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} - \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} \right],
$$

$$
\delta i _ {2 3} ^ {\boldsymbol {h}} = \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \left[ \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {(2)} - \mathbf {k} ^ {(2)} \right] (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h}.
$$

we notice that

$$
i _ {1} ^ {n} - i _ {1} = \delta i _ {1 1} ^ {f, \pmb {f}} + \delta i _ {1 2} ^ {f, \pmb {f}}, \qquad i _ {2} ^ {n} - i _ {2} = \delta i _ {2 1} ^ {\pmb {f}} + \delta i _ {2 2} ^ {\pmb {f}} + \delta i _ {2 3} ^ {\pmb {f}},
$$

so we only need to bound these delta terms. below we state a lemma for this general setup. note that  $\pmb{f}$  satisfies the assumption on the random vector therein, because by the law of large numbers  $n^{-1}\| \pmb {f}\| ^2 = (1 + o_{n,\mathbb{p}}(1))\| f\| _l^2$  , so  $\| \pmb {f}\| \leq c_1\sqrt{n}$  with high probability.

lemma 3. suppose that, for  $c_1 > 0$  a constant, we have  $\| g \|_{l^2} \leq c_1$ , and that  $h \in \mathbb{r}^n$  is a random vector that satisfies  $\| h \| \leq c_1 \sqrt{n}$  with high probability. then, there exists a constant  $c' > 0$  such that the following bounds hold with high probability.

$$
\left\| \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) g (\boldsymbol {x}) \right] \right\| \leq \frac {c ^ {\prime}}{\sqrt {n}}, \tag {67}
$$

$$
\left\| \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {(2)} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {\text {o p}} \leq \frac {c ^ {\prime}}{n}, \tag {68}
$$

$$
\left\| \mathbf {k} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} \right\| \leq c ^ {\prime} \sqrt {n}, \tag {69}
$$

$$
\left\| \mathbf {k} _ {n} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} \right\| \leq c ^ {\prime} \sqrt {n}, \tag {70}
$$

$$
\left\| \mathbf {k} _ {n} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} - \mathbf {k} _ {n} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} \right\| \leq c ^ {\prime} \eta \sqrt {n}. \tag {71}
$$

here, we denote  $\eta = \sqrt{n(\log(nnd))^{c'} / (nd)}$ . as a consequence, we have, with high probability,

$$
\max  \left\{\left| \delta i _ {1 1} ^ {g, f} \right|, \left| \delta i _ {2 1} ^ {h} \right|, \left| \delta i _ {2 2} ^ {h} \right| \right\} \leq c ^ {\prime} \eta . \tag {72}
$$

we handle the other two terms  $\delta i_{12}^{g,h}$  and  $\delta i_{23}^{\pmb{h}}$  in a different way. denote

$$
\boldsymbol {v} = (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}, \quad \widetilde {h} (\boldsymbol {x}) = \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h}.
$$

the function  $\widetilde{h}$  satisfies  $\| \widetilde{h}\|_{l^2} \leq c\| \pmb{h}\|^2 / n$  with high probability by the following lemma, which we will prove in section b.3.

lemma 4. define  $\mathbf{k}^{(2)}\coloneqq \mathbb{e}_{\boldsymbol{x}}[\mathbf{k}(\cdot ,\boldsymbol {x})\mathbf{k}(\cdot ,\boldsymbol{x})^{\top}]\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times n}$  or, equivalently,

$$
k _ {i j} ^ {(2)} = \left(\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} [ k (\boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}) k (\boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {j}) ]\right) _ {i, j \leq n}. \tag {73}
$$

then, with high probability,

$$
\left\| \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} ^ {(2)} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {c}{n}, \tag {74}
$$

$$
\left\| \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) f (\boldsymbol {x}) \right] \right\| \leq \frac {c}{\sqrt {n}} \| f \| _ {l ^ {2}}. \tag {75}
$$

we can rewrite  $\delta i_{12}^{g,h}$  and  $\delta i_{23}^h$  as

$$
\delta i _ {1 2} ^ {g, \boldsymbol {h}} = \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x})) g (\boldsymbol {x}) \right],
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \delta i _ {2 3} ^ {\boldsymbol {h}} = 2 \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x})) \widetilde {h} (\boldsymbol {x}) \right] + \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x})) (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x})) ^ {\top} \right] \boldsymbol {v} \\ =: 2 \delta i _ {2 3 1} ^ {\boldsymbol {h}} + \delta i _ {2 3 2} ^ {\boldsymbol {h}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

note that  $\delta i_{232}^{\pmb{h}}$  is always nonnegative. we calculate and bound  $\mathbb{e}_{\pmb{w}}[(\delta i_{12}^{g,\pmb{h}})^{2}]$ ,  $\mathbb{e}_{\pmb{w}}[(\delta i_{231}^{\pmb{h}})^{2}]$ , and  $\mathbb{e}_{\pmb{w}}[\delta i_{232}^{\pmb{h}}]$ , so that we obtain bounds on  $\delta i_{12}^{g,\pmb{h}}$  and  $\delta i_{23}^{\pmb{h}}$  with high probability.

lemma 5. suppose that, for  $c_1 > 0$  a constant, we have  $\| g \|_{l^2} \leq c_1$ , and that  $\pmb{h} \in \mathbb{r}^n$  is a random vector that satisfies  $\| \pmb{h} \| \leq c_1 \sqrt{n}$  with high probability.

let  $z_1, z_2, z$  be independent copies of  $x$ . then we have

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ \left(\delta i _ {1 2} ^ {g, \boldsymbol {h}}\right) ^ {2} \right] \leq \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {h} _ {1} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h}, \tag {76}
$$

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ \left(\delta i _ {2 3 1} ^ {\boldsymbol {h}}\right) ^ {2} \right] \leq \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {h} _ {2} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h}, \tag {77}
$$

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ \delta i _ {2 3 2} ^ {\boldsymbol {h}} \right] \leq \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {h} _ {3} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h}, \tag {78}
$$

where  $\mathbf{h}_j = (h_j(\pmb{x}_i, \pmb{x}_j))_{i,j \leq n} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times n}$  ( $j = 1, 2, 3$ ) are given by

$$
h _ {1} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {2}) = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {z} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {z} _ {2}, \boldsymbol {w}} \big [ \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {z} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {2}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {z} _ {2}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) \frac {\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {1} , \boldsymbol {z} _ {1} \rangle}{d} \frac {\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {2} , \boldsymbol {z} _ {2} \rangle}{d} g (\boldsymbol {z} _ {1}) g (\boldsymbol {z} _ {2}) \big ]
$$

$$
h _ {2} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {2}) = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {z} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {z} _ {2}, \boldsymbol {w}} \left[ \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {z} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {2}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {z} _ {2}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) \frac {\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {1} , \boldsymbol {z} _ {1} \rangle}{d} \frac {\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {2} , \boldsymbol {z} _ {2} \rangle}{d} \widetilde {h} (\boldsymbol {z} _ {1}) \widetilde {h} (\boldsymbol {z} _ {2}) \right]
$$

$$
h _ {3} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {2}) = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {z}, \boldsymbol {w}} \left[ \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {2}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) [ \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {z}, \boldsymbol {w} \rangle) ] ^ {2} \frac {\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {1} , \boldsymbol {z} \rangle}{d} \frac {\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {2} , \boldsymbol {z} \rangle}{d} \right].
$$

moreover, it holds that with high probability,

$$
\mathbf {h} _ {1} \preceq \frac {c}{d} \mathbf {k}, \quad \mathbf {h} _ {2} \preceq \frac {c}{d} \mathbf {k}, \quad \mathbf {h} _ {3} \preceq \frac {c}{d} \mathbf {k}.
$$

as a consequence, with high probability, we have

$$
\left| \delta i _ {1 2} ^ {g, h} \right| \leq c \sqrt {\frac {n \log n}{n d}}, \quad \left| \delta i _ {2 3} ^ {h} \right| \leq c \sqrt {\frac {n \log n}{n d}}.
$$

in lemma 3 and 5, we set  $\pmb{h} = \pmb{f}$  and  $g = f$ . by the conclusions of these two lemmas, we will obtain the bound on the bias term in theorem 8.1. we defer the proofs of the two lemmas to the appendix.

# 9 conclusions

we studied the neural tangent model associated to two-layer neural networks, focusing on its generalization properties in a regime in which the sample size  $n$ , the dimension  $d$ , and the number of neurons  $n$  are all large and polynomially related ( $c_0d \leq n \leq d^{1/c_0}$  for some small constant  $c_0 > 0$ ), while in the overparametrized regime  $nd \gg n$ . we assumed an isotropic model for the data  $(\pmb{x}_i)_{i \leq n}$ , and noisy label  $(y_i)_{i \leq n}$  with a general target  $y_i = f_*(\pmb{x}_i) + \varepsilon_i$  (with  $\varepsilon_i$  independent noise).

as a fundamental technical step, we obtained a characterization of the eigenstructure of the empirical nt kernel  $\mathbf{k}_n$  in the overparametrized regime. in particular for non-polynomial activations, we showed that, the minimum eigenvalue of  $\mathbf{k}_n$  is bounded away from zero, as soon as  $nd / (\log nd)^c\geq n$ . further, for  $d^{\ell}(\log d)^{c_0}\leq n\leq d^{\ell +1} / (\log d)^{c_0}$ ,  $\ell \in \mathbb{n}$ ,  $\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(\mathbf{k}_n)$  concentrates around a value that is explicitly given in terms of the hermite decomposition of the activation.

an immediate corollary is that, as soon as  $nd \geq n(\log d)^{c}$ , the neural network can exactly interpolate arbitrary labels.

our most important result is a characterization of the test error of minimum  $\ell_{2}$ -norm nt regression. we prove that, for  $nd / (\log nd)^{c} \geq n$  the test error is close to the one of the  $n = \infty$  limit (i.e. of kernel ridgeless regression with respect to the expected kernel). the latter is in turn well approximated by polynomial regression with respect degree- $\ell$  polynomials as long as  $d^{\ell}(\log d)^{c_0} \leq n \leq d^{\ell + 1} / (\log d)^{c_0}$ .

our analysis offers several insight to statistical practice:

1. nt models provide an effective randomized approximation to kernel methods. indeed their statistical properties are analogous to the one of more standard random features methods [rr08], when we compare them at fixed number of parameters [mmm21]. however, the computational complexity at prediction time of nt models is smaller than the one of random feature models if we keep the same number of parameters.  
2. the additional error due to the finite number of hidden units is roughly bounded by  $\sqrt{n / (nd)}$ .  
3. in high dimensions, the nonlinearity in the activation function produces an effective 'self-induced' regularization (diagonal term in the kernel) and as a consequence interpolation can be optimal.

finally, our characterization of generalization error applies to two-layer neural networks (under specific initializations) as long as the neural tangent theory provides a good approximation for their behavior. in section 5 we discussed sufficient conditions for this to be the case, but we do not expect these conditions to be sharp.

# a additional numerical experiment

the setup of the experiment is similar to the second experiment in section 2: we fit a linear model  $y_{i} = \langle \pmb{x}_{i},\pmb{\beta}_{*}\rangle +\varepsilon_{i}$  where covariates satisfy  $\pmb {x}_i\sim \mathrm{unif}(\mathbb{s}^{d - 1}(\sqrt{d}))$  and the noises satisfy  $\varepsilon_{i}\sim \mathcal{n}(0,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})$ ,  $\sigma_{\varepsilon} = 0.5$ . we fix  $n = 800$  and  $d = 500$ , and vary the sample size  $n\in \{100,200,\dots ,900,1000,1500,\dots ,4000\}$ .

figure 5 illustrates the relation between the nt, 2-layer nn, and polynomial ridge regression. we train a two-layer neural network with relu activations using the initialization strategy of [cob19]. namely, we draw  $(a_k^0)_{k\leq n}\sim_{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}\mathcal{n}(0,1)$ ,  $(\pmb {w}_k^0)_{k\leq n}\sim_{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}\mathcal{n}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{i}_d)$  and set  $\widetilde{a}_k^0 = -a_k^0$ $\widetilde{\pmb{w}}_k^0 = \pmb{w}_k^0$ . we use adam optimizer to train the two-layer neural network  $f(\pmb {x}) = \sum_{k = 1}^{n}a_{k}\sigma (\langle \pmb{w}_{k},\pmb {x}\rangle) + \sum_{\ell = 1}^{n}\widetilde{a}_{k}\sigma (\langle \widetilde{\pmb{w}}_{k},\pmb {x}\rangle)$  with parameters  $(a_{k},\widetilde{a}_{k},\pmb{w}_{\ell},\widetilde{\pmb{w}}_{\ell})_{k\leq n}$  initialized by  $(a_k^0,\widetilde{a}_k^0,\pmb {w}_k^0,\widetilde{\pmb{w}}_k^0)_{k\leq n}$ . this guarantees that the output is zero at initialization and the parameter scale is much larger than the default, so that we are in the lazy training regime. we compare its generalization error with the one of prr, and with the theoretical prediction of remark 4. the agreement is excellent.

![](images/5db78abbe5de799a5193dbbe58238a6d32d1183abccac563598323f89bbeb665.jpg)  
figure 5: test/generalization errors of nt, poly, and two-layer neural network (2-nn). we fix  $n = 800$ ,  $d = 500$  and vary  $n$ . for each noise level  $\sigma_{\varepsilon} \in \{0.01, 0.1, 0.3, 0.5\}$  (which corresponds to one color), we plot three curves that represent the test errors for nt, poly, and 2-nn. all curves of the same color behave similarly.

# b generalization error: proof of theorem 3.3

this section finishes the proof of our main result, theorem 3.3, which was initiated in section 8. we use definitions and notations introduced in that section.

# b.1 proof of theorem 8.1: variance term and cross term

by homogeneity we can and will assume, without loss of generality,  $\| f\|_{l^2} = \sigma_\varepsilon = 1$

we handle  $e_{\mathrm{var}}^{n}$  and  $e_{\mathrm{cross}}^{n}$  in a way similar to that of  $e_{\mathrm{bias}}^{n}$  (mostly following the same proof

with  $\pmb{h}$  set to  $\pmb{\varepsilon}$  instead of  $\pmb{f}$ ). first we observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \varepsilon^ {\top} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {(2)} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \varepsilon - \varepsilon^ {\top} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} ^ {(2)} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \varepsilon \\ = \left[ \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} - \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} \right] \cdot \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {(2)} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \cdot \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \\ + \varepsilon^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} \cdot \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {(2)} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \cdot \left[ \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \varepsilon - \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \varepsilon \right] \\ + \varepsilon^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \left[ \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {(2)} - \mathbf {k} ^ {(2)} \right] (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \varepsilon \\ =: \delta j _ {1} + \delta j _ {2} + \delta j _ {3}. \\ \end{array}
$$

note that  $n^{-1}\| \pmb {\varepsilon}\| ^2 = (1 + o_{d,\mathbb{p}}(1))\sigma_\varepsilon^2$  by the law of large numbers, and therefore  $\| \pmb {\varepsilon}\| \leq c_1\sqrt{n}$  with high probability. applying lemma 3 with  $\pmb{h}$  set to  $\pmb{\varepsilon}$ , we obtain the following holds with high probability.

$$
\left\| \mathbf {k} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \right\| \leq c \sqrt {n}, \tag {79}
$$

$$
\left\| \mathbf {k} _ {n} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \right\| \leq c \sqrt {n}, \tag {80}
$$

$$
\left\| \mathbf {k} _ {n} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} - \mathbf {k} _ {n} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \right\| \leq c \eta \sqrt {n}. \tag {81}
$$

this implies that  $|\delta j_1| \leq c\eta$  and  $|\delta j_2| \leq c\eta$  w.h.p. moreover, denoting

$$
\boldsymbol {v} = (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}, \quad \widetilde {h} (\boldsymbol {x}) = \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon},
$$

we express  $\delta j_{3}$  as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \delta j _ {3} = 2 \pmb {v} ^ {\top} \mathbb {e} _ {\pmb {x}} \big [ (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \pmb {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \pmb {x})) \widetilde {h} (\pmb {x}) \big ] + \pmb {v} ^ {\top} \mathbb {e} _ {\pmb {x}} \big [ (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \pmb {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \pmb {x})) (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \pmb {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \pmb {x})) ^ {\top} \big ] \pmb {v} \\ =: 2 \delta j _ {3 1} + \delta j _ {3 2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

applying lemma 5 in which we set  $\pmb{h} = \pmb{\varepsilon}$ , we obtain w.h.p.,

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ (\delta j _ {3 1}) ^ {2} \right] \leq \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\top} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {h} _ {2} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \leq \frac {c n}{n d}
$$

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ \delta j _ {3 2} \right] \leq \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\top} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {h} _ {3} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \leq \frac {c n}{n d}.
$$

this implies that  $|\delta j_3| \leq c\eta$  w.h.p. as well. this proves the bound on the variance term in theorem 8.1.

now for the cross term, we observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} e _ {\mathrm {c r o s s}} ^ {n} - e _ {\mathrm {c r o s s}} = \left[ \varepsilon^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) f (\boldsymbol {x}) \right] - \varepsilon^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) f (\boldsymbol {x}) \right] \right] \\ - \left[ \varepsilon^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {(2)} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f} - \varepsilon^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} ^ {(2)} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f} \right] \\ =: \widetilde {\delta j} _ {1} - \widetilde {\delta j} _ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

for the first term  $\widetilde{\delta j_1}$ , we further decompose:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widetilde {\delta j _ {1}} = \left[ \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} - \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} \right] \cdot \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \big [ \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) f (\boldsymbol {x}) \big ], \\ + \varepsilon^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \big [ (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x})) f (\boldsymbol {x}) \big ] =: \widetilde {\delta j} _ {1 1} + \widetilde {\delta j} _ {1 2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

from eqs. (67) and (71) in lemma 3 (setting  $g = f$  and  $\pmb{h} = \pmb{\varepsilon}$ ), we have  $|\delta \widetilde{j}_{11}| \leq c\eta$  w.h.p. by lemma 5 eq. (76) (setting  $\pmb{h}$  to  $\pmb{\varepsilon}$ ), w.h.p.,

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} [ (\widetilde {\delta j _ {1 2}}) ^ {2} ] \leq \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {h} _ {1} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \leq \frac {c n}{n d},
$$

so with high probability  $|\widetilde{\delta j}_{12}| \leq c\eta$ . thus we proved  $|\widetilde{\delta j}_1| \leq c\eta$ .

finally, we further decompose  $\delta j_{2}$  as follows.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widetilde {\delta j _ {2}} = \left[ \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} - \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} \right] \cdot \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {(2)} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \cdot \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f}, \\ + \pmb {\varepsilon} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} \cdot \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {(2)} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \cdot \left[ \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \pmb {f} - \mathbf {k} _ {n} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \pmb {f} \right], \\ + \varepsilon^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \left[ \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {(2)} - \mathbf {k} ^ {(2)} \right] (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f} \\ =: \widetilde {\delta j} _ {2 1} + \widetilde {\delta j} _ {2 2} + \widetilde {\delta j} _ {2 3}. \\ \end{array}
$$

by lemma 3 (in which we set  $\pmb{h}$  to  $\pmb{f}$ ) and eqs. (79)-(81), we have  $|\widetilde{\delta j}_{21}| \leq c\eta$  and  $|\widetilde{\delta j}_{22}| \leq c\eta$  w.h.p. moreover, denoting

$$
\boldsymbol {v} _ {1} = \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f}, \quad \widetilde {h} _ {1} (\boldsymbol {x}) = \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) ^ {\top} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f}
$$

$$
\pmb {v} _ {2} = (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \pmb {\varepsilon}, \qquad \widetilde {h} _ {2} (\pmb {x}) = \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \pmb {x}) ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \pmb {\varepsilon},
$$

we express  $\widetilde{\delta j}_{23}$  as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widetilde {\delta j} _ {2 3} = \boldsymbol {v} _ {2} ^ {\top} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x})) \widetilde {h} _ {1} (\boldsymbol {x}) \right] + \boldsymbol {v} _ {1} ^ {\top} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x})) \widetilde {h} _ {2} (\boldsymbol {x}) \right] \\ + \boldsymbol {v} _ {2} ^ {\top} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \big [ (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x})) (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x})) ^ {\top} \big ] \boldsymbol {v} _ {1} \\ =: \widetilde {\delta j} _ {2 3 1} + \widetilde {\delta j} _ {2 3 1} ^ {\prime} + \widetilde {\delta j} _ {2 3 2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

in lemma 5 eq. (76), we set  $g = \widetilde{h}_1, \pmb{h} = \pmb{\varepsilon}$  to obtain  $\mathbb{e}_{\pmb{w}}[(\widetilde{\delta j}_{231})^2] \leq cn / (nd)$ ; and we set  $g = \widetilde{h}_2, \pmb{h} = \pmb{f}$  to obtain  $\mathbb{e}_{\pmb{w}}[(\widetilde{\delta j}_{231}')^2] \leq cn / (nd)$ . for  $\widetilde{\delta j}_{232}$ , we use

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widetilde {\delta j} _ {2 3 2} = \left(\boldsymbol {v} _ {1} + \boldsymbol {v} _ {2}\right) ^ {\top} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \left(\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x})\right) \left(\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x})\right) ^ {\top} \right] \left(\boldsymbol {v} _ {1} + \boldsymbol {v} _ {2}\right) \\ - \pmb {v} _ {1} ^ {\top} \mathbb {e} _ {\pmb {x}} \big [ (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \pmb {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \pmb {x})) (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \pmb {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \pmb {x})) ^ {\top} \big ] \pmb {v} _ {1} \\ - \pmb {v} _ {2} ^ {\top} \mathbb {e} _ {\pmb {x}} \big [ (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \pmb {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \pmb {x})) (\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \pmb {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \pmb {x})) ^ {\top} \big ] \pmb {v} _ {2} \\ \leq \left(\boldsymbol {v} _ {1} + \boldsymbol {v} _ {2}\right) ^ {\top} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \left(\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x})\right) \left(\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x}) - \mathbf {k} (\cdot , \boldsymbol {x})\right) ^ {\top} \right] \left(\boldsymbol {v} _ {1} + \boldsymbol {v} _ {2}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

note that  $\| f + \varepsilon \| \leq c\sqrt{n}$  w.h.p., so applying lemma 5 eq. (78) with  $\pmb{h} = \pmb{f} + \pmb{\varepsilon}$  leads to  $\mathbb{e}_{\pmb{w}}[\widetilde{\delta j_{232}}] \leq cn/(nd)$  w.h.p. this proves  $|\widetilde{\delta j_{23}}| \leq cn/(nd)$  w.h.p. and therefore  $|\widetilde{\delta j_2}| \leq c\eta$  w.h.p. hence, we have proved the bound on the cross term in theorem 8.1.

# b.2 proof of lemma 3

throughout this subsection, given a sequence of random variables  $\xi_{d}$ , we write  $\xi_{d} = \check{o}_{d,\mathbb{p}}(1)$  if and only if for any  $\varepsilon > 0$  and  $\beta > 0$ , we have  $\lim_{d \to 0} d^{\beta} \mathbb{p}(|\xi_d| > \varepsilon) = 0$ . we also assume that

$$
\frac {d ^ {\ell + 1}}{(\log n) ^ {c}} \geq n \geq d ^ {\ell} (\log n) ^ {c}, \quad n \leq \frac {n d}{(\log (n d)) ^ {c}}
$$

for a sufficiently large  $c > 0$  such that we can use the following inequalities by lemma 2 and theorem 3.2:

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = \check {o} _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1), \quad \left\| n ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} - \mathbf {i} _ {n} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = \check {o} _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1), \quad \left\| \mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2} \left(\mathbf {k} _ {n} - \mathbf {k}\right) \mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = \check {o} _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1). \tag {82}
$$

in section c we will refine our analysis to eliminate logarithmic factors for  $\ell = 1$ .

we start with the easiest bounds in lemma 3.

proof of lemma 3, eqs. (69)-(71). the first two bounds follow from the observation

$$
\mathbf {k} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} = \boldsymbol {h} - \lambda (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h},
$$

$$
\mathbf {k} _ {n} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \pmb {h} = \pmb {h} - \lambda (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \pmb {h}
$$

from the invertibility of  $\mathbf{k}$  and  $\mathbf{k}_n$ , with high probability,

$$
\left\| \lambda (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {\lambda}{c + \lambda}, \quad \left\| \lambda (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {\lambda}{c + \lambda}.
$$

we conclude that eq. (69) and (70) hold with high probability.

next, we will show that w.h.p.,

$$
\left\| \mathbf {k} _ {n} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} - \mathbf {k} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq c \sqrt {\eta}, \tag {83}
$$

$$
\left\| \left(\mathbf {k} _ {n} - \mathbf {k}\right) \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} \right\| \leq c \sqrt {\eta n}. \tag {84}
$$

once these are proved, then, w.h.p.,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbf {k} _ {n} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} - \mathbf {k} _ {n} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} \right\| \leq \left\| \mathbf {k} _ {n} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} - \mathbf {k} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \cdot \| \boldsymbol {h} \| \\ + \left\| (\mathbf {k} _ {n} - \mathbf {k}) (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \pmb {h} \right\| \leq c \sqrt {\eta n}. \\ \end{array}
$$

in order to show (83), we observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {k} _ {n} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} - \mathbf {k} \left(\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {- 1} \\ = - \lambda (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} + \lambda (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \\ = \lambda (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} (\mathbf {k} _ {n} - \mathbf {k}) (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \\ = \lambda (\lambda {\bf i} _ {n} + {\bf k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} {\bf k} _ {n} ^ {1 / 2} {\bf k} _ {n} ^ {- 1 / 2} {\bf k} ^ {1 / 2} {\bf k} ^ {- 1 / 2} ({\bf k} _ {n} - {\bf k}) {\bf k} ^ {- 1 / 2} {\bf k} ^ {1 / 2} (\lambda {\bf i} _ {n} + {\bf k}) ^ {- 1}. \\ \end{array}
$$

from theorem 3.2, we know that  $\| \mathbf{k}^{-1 / 2}(\mathbf{k}_n - \mathbf{k})\mathbf{k}^{-1 / 2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq \sqrt{\eta}$  w.h.p. it also implies

$$
\| \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {k} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} = \| \mathbf {k} ^ {1 / 2} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = \| (\mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {k} _ {n} \mathbf {k} ^ {- 1 / 2}) ^ {- 1} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq (1 - \sqrt {\eta}) ^ {- 1}.
$$

also, we notice that

$$
\left\| \lambda^ {1 / 2} \mathbf {k} ^ {1 / 2} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \max _ {i} \frac {\lambda^ {1 / 2} [ \lambda_ {i} (\mathbf {k}) ] ^ {1 / 2}}{\lambda + \lambda_ {i} (\mathbf {k})} \leq \frac {1}{2},
$$

$$
\left\| \lambda^ {1 / 2} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {k} _ {n} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \max _ {i} \frac {\lambda^ {1 / 2} [ \lambda_ {i} (\mathbf {k} _ {n}) ] ^ {1 / 2}}{\lambda + \lambda_ {i} (\mathbf {k} _ {n})} \leq \frac {1}{2},
$$

these bounds then lead to the desired bound in (83). in order to show (84), we recall the notation  $\pmb{v} = (\lambda \mathbf{i}_n + \mathbf{k})^{-1}\pmb{h}$ . we also write  $\mathbf{k}_n = n^{-1}\sum_{k\leq n}\mathbf{k}^{(k)}$  where  $(\mathbf{k}^{(k)})_{k\leq n}$  are independent conditional on  $(\pmb{x}_i)_{i\leq n}$  and  $\mathbb{e}_{\pmb{w}}[\mathbf{k}^{(k)}] = \mathbf{k}$ . we calculate

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ \left\| (\mathbf {k} _ {n} - \mathbf {k}) \boldsymbol {v} \right\| ^ {2} \right] = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \langle \boldsymbol {v}, (\mathbf {k} _ {n} - \mathbf {k}) ^ {2} \boldsymbol {v} \rangle = \frac {1}{n ^ {2}} \sum_ {k, k ^ {\prime} \leq n} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \langle \boldsymbol {v}, (\mathbf {k} ^ {(k)} - \mathbf {k}) (\mathbf {k} ^ {(k ^ {\prime})} - \mathbf {k}) \boldsymbol {v} \rangle (85) \\ = \frac {1}{n ^ {2}} \sum_ {k \leq n} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left\langle \boldsymbol {v}, \left(\mathbf {k} ^ {(k)} - \mathbf {k}\right) ^ {2} \boldsymbol {v} \right\rangle (86) \\ \stackrel {(i)} {\leq} \frac {1}{n ^ {2}} \sum_ {k \leq n} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {v}} \langle \boldsymbol {v}, (\mathbf {k} ^ {(k)}) ^ {2} \boldsymbol {v} \rangle (87) \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $(i)$  is due to

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ (\mathbf {k} ^ {(k)} - \mathbf {k}) ^ {2} \right] = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ (\mathbf {k} ^ {(k)}) ^ {2} - \mathbf {k} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {k} - \mathbf {k k} ^ {(k)} + \mathbf {k} ^ {2} \right] = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ (\mathbf {k} ^ {(k)}) ^ {2} \right] - \mathbf {k} ^ {2} \preceq \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ (\mathbf {k} ^ {(k)}) ^ {2} \right].
$$

recall the notations in the proof of theorem 3.2, cf. eqs. (55), (56).

we denote the indicator variable  $\xi = \mathbf{1}\{\left|\langle \pmb{x}_i,\pmb{w}_k\rangle \right| \leq c_0\log (nnd), \forall i,k\}$ . if  $c_0$  is sufficiently large, we have  $\mathbb{e}_{\pmb{w}}\{\| \mathbf{k}^{(k)}(1 - \xi)\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2\} \leq c / (nnd)^2$ . therefore

$$
\mathbf {k} ^ {(k)} = \mathbf {k} ^ {(k)} \boldsymbol {\xi} + \mathbf {k} ^ {(k)} (1 - \boldsymbol {\xi}) = d ^ {- 1} \mathbf {d} _ {k} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \mathbf {d} _ {k} \boldsymbol {\xi} + \mathbf {k} ^ {(k)} (1 - \boldsymbol {\xi}), \qquad \mathrm {a n d t h u s}
$$

$$
\pmb {v} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {k} ^ {(k)}) ^ {2} \pmb {v} = \pmb {v} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {k} ^ {(k)}) ^ {2} \pmb {v} \xi + \pmb {v} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {k} ^ {(k)}) ^ {2} \pmb {v} (1 - \xi) \leq \pmb {v} ^ {\top} (d ^ {- 1} \mathbf {d} _ {k} \pmb {x} \pmb {x} ^ {\top} \mathbf {d} _ {k}) ^ {2} \pmb {v} + \frac {c \| \pmb {v} \| ^ {2}}{n ^ {2} n ^ {2} d ^ {2}}.
$$

now we work on the event  $\| x\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2(\sqrt{n} +\sqrt{d})$ , which happens with high probability. continuing from eq. (87), we get:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \big [ \big \| (\mathbf {k} _ {n} - \mathbf {k}) \boldsymbol {v} \big \| ^ {2} \big ] \leq \frac {1}{n ^ {2} d ^ {2}} \sum_ {k \leq n} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \big [ \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} \mathbf {d} _ {k} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \mathbf {d} _ {k} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \mathbf {d} _ {k} \boldsymbol {v} \big ] + \frac {c \| \boldsymbol {v} \| ^ {2}}{n ^ {2} n ^ {2} d ^ {2}} \\ \leq \frac {n (\log (n n d)) ^ {c}}{n ^ {2} d ^ {2}} \sum_ {k \leq n} \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \left[ \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} \mathbf {d} _ {k} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \mathbf {d} _ {k} \boldsymbol {v} \right] + \frac {c \| \boldsymbol {v} \| ^ {2}}{n ^ {2} n ^ {2} d ^ {2}} \\ = \frac {n (\log (n n d)) ^ {c}}{n d} \mathbf {v} ^ {\top} \mathbf {k} \mathbf {v} + \frac {c \| \mathbf {v} \| ^ {2}}{n ^ {2} n ^ {2} d ^ {2}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

finally, since  $\mathbf{k} \preceq \lambda \mathbf{i}_n + \mathbf{k}$ , w.h.p.,

$$
\boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} \mathbf {k} \boldsymbol {v} \leq \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) (\lambda \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \mathbf {k}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} \leq \frac {1}{c + \lambda} \| \boldsymbol {h} \| ^ {2} \leq c n,
$$

$$
\| \boldsymbol {v} \| ^ {2} \leq \frac {1}{c + \lambda} \| \boldsymbol {h} \| ^ {2} \leq c n.
$$

we have shown that

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {w}} \big [ \big \| (\mathbf {k} _ {n} - \mathbf {k}) \boldsymbol {v} \big \| ^ {2} \big ] \leq \frac {n ^ {2} (\log (n n d)) ^ {c}}{n d}.
$$

by markov's inequality, we then obtain  $\| (\mathbf{k}_n - \mathbf{k})\pmb {v}\| \leq \sqrt{\eta n}$  w.h.p., as claimed in eq. (84).

before proving the more difficult inequalities (67), (68), we establish some useful properties of the eigenstructure of  $\mathbf{k}_n$ . for convenience, we write  $\mathbf{d}_1 = \mathbf{d}_2 \cdot (1 + \check{o}_{d,\mathbb{p}}(1))$  if  $\mathbf{d}_1, \mathbf{d}_2$  are diagonal matrices and  $\max_i \left| (\mathbf{d}_1)_{ii} / (\mathbf{d}_2)_{ii} - 1 \right| = \check{o}_{d,\mathbb{p}}(1)$ ; we also write  $\mathbf{d}_1 = \mathbf{d}_2 + \check{o}_{d,\mathbb{p}}(1)$  if  $\mathbf{d}_1, \mathbf{d}_2$  are diagonal matrices and  $\max_i \left| (\mathbf{d}_1)_{ii} - (\mathbf{d}_2)_{ii} \right| = \check{o}_{d,\mathbb{p}}(1)$ . denote  $d = \sum_{k \leq \ell} b(d, k)$ .

lemma 6 (kernel eigenvalue structure). the eigen-decomposition of  $\mathbf{k} - \gamma_{> \ell} \mathbf{i}_n$  and  $\mathbf{k}_n - \gamma_{> \ell} \mathbf{i}_n$  can be written in the following form:

$$
\mathbf {k} - \gamma_ {> \ell} \mathbf {i} _ {n} = \mathbf {u d u} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {\delta} ^ {(\text {r e s})}, \tag {88}
$$

$$
\mathbf {k} _ {n} - \gamma_ {> \ell} \mathbf {i} _ {n} = \mathbf {u} _ {n} \mathbf {d} _ {n} \mathbf {u} _ {n} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {n} ^ {(\text {r e s})}, \tag {89}
$$

where  $\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}_n\in \mathbb{r}^{d\times d}$  are diagonal matrices that contain  $d$  eigenvalues of  $\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}_n$  respectively, columns of  $\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}_n\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times d}$  are the corresponding eigenvectors and  $\pmb{\delta}^{\mathrm{(res)}},\pmb{\delta}_{n}^{\mathrm{(res)}}$  correspond to the other eigenvectors (in particular  $\pmb{\delta}^{\mathrm{(res)}}\mathbf{u} = \pmb{\delta}_{n}^{\mathrm{(res)}}\mathbf{u}_{n} = \mathbf{0})$

the eigenvalues in  $\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}_n$  have the following structure.

$$
\mathbf {d} = \operatorname {d i a g} \left(\gamma_ {0} n, \underbrace {\frac {\gamma_ {1} n}{d} , \dots , \frac {\gamma_ {1} n}{d}} _ {d}, \dots , \underbrace {\frac {\gamma_ {\ell} (\ell !) n}{d ^ {\ell}} , \dots , \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} (\ell !) n}{d ^ {\ell}}} _ {b (d, \ell)}\right) \cdot (1 + \check {o} _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1)) + \check {o} _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1), \tag {90}
$$

$$
\mathbf {d} _ {n} = \operatorname {d i a g} \left(\gamma_ {0} n, \underbrace {\frac {\gamma_ {1} n}{d} , \dots , \frac {\gamma_ {1} n}{d}} _ {d}, \dots , \underbrace {\frac {\gamma_ {\ell} (\ell !) n}{d ^ {\ell}} , \dots , \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} (\ell !) n}{d ^ {\ell}}} _ {b (d, \ell)}\right) \cdot (1 + \check {o} _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1)) + \check {o} _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1). \tag {91}
$$

moreover, the remaining components satisfy  $\| \pmb{\delta}^{(\mathrm{res})}\|_{\mathrm{op}} = \check{o}_{d,\mathbb{p}}(1)$  and  $\| \pmb{\delta}_n^{(\mathrm{res})}\|_{\mathrm{op}} = \check{o}_{d,\mathbb{p}}(1)$ .

proof of lemma 6. for convenience, for a symmetric matrix  $\mathbf{a}$ , we denote by  $\lambda(\mathbf{a})$  the vector of eigenvalues of  $\mathbf{a}$ . we write  $\lambda(\mathbf{a}_1) = \lambda(\mathbf{a}_2) \cdot (1 + \check{o}_{d,\mathbb{p}}(1))$  if  $\max_i |\lambda_i(\mathbf{a}_1) / \lambda_i(\mathbf{a}_2) - 1| = \check{o}_{d,\mathbb{p}}(1)$ . from lemma 2, we can decompose  $\mathbf{k}$  as

$$
\mathbf {k} = \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top} + \gamma_ {> \ell} \mathbf {i} _ {n} + \boldsymbol {\delta}.
$$

we claim that

$$
\boldsymbol {\lambda} \left(\psi_ {\leq \ell} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top}\right) = \left(\gamma_ {0} n, \underbrace {\frac {\gamma_ {1} n}{d} , \dots , \frac {\gamma_ {1} n}{d}} _ {d}, \dots , \underbrace {\frac {\gamma_ {\ell} (\ell !) n}{d ^ {\ell}} , \dots , \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} (\ell !) n}{d ^ {\ell}}} _ {b (d, \ell)}, \underbrace {0 , \dots , 0} _ {n - d}\right) \cdot \left(1 + \check {\sigma} _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1)\right). \tag {92}
$$

to prove this claim, first note that (82) implies  $n^{-1/2} \|\psi_{\leq \ell}\|_{\mathrm{op}} = 1 + \check{o}_{d,\mathbb{p}}(1)$ . observe that  $\psi_{\leq \ell} \lambda_{\leq \ell}^2 \psi_{\leq \ell}^\top$  has rank at most  $d$ . define  $q \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times d}$  such that its columns are the left singular vectors of  $\psi_{\leq \ell}$ . we only need to show

$$
\boldsymbol {\lambda} \left(\boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q}\right) = \left(\gamma_ {0} n, \underbrace {\frac {\gamma_ {1} n}{d} , \dots , \frac {\gamma_ {1} n}{d}} _ {d}, \dots , \underbrace {\frac {\gamma_ {\ell} (\ell !) n}{d ^ {\ell}} , \dots , \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} (\ell !) n}{d ^ {\ell}}} _ {b (d, \ell)}\right) \cdot (1 + \check {o} _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1)). \tag {93}
$$

in order to prove the above claim, we use the eigenvalue min-max principle to express the  $k$ -th eigenvalue of  $\pmb{q}^{\top}\pmb{\psi}_{\leq \ell}\pmb{\lambda}_{\leq \ell}^{2}\pmb{\psi}_{\leq \ell}^{\top}\pmb{q}$ . we sort the diagonal entries of  $\pmb{\lambda}_{\leq \ell}^{2}$  in the descending order and denote the ranks by  $s_1,\ldots ,s_d$  (break ties arbitrarily). define the subspaces  $\mathcal{v}_1 = \mathrm{span}\{e_{s_1},\dots ,e_{s_k}\}$ ,  $\mathcal{v}_2 = \mathrm{span}\{e_{s_k},\dots ,e_{s_d}\} \subset \mathbb{r}^d$  and  $\mathcal{v}_1' = \mathrm{span}\{\pmb {u}\in \mathbb{r}^d:\pmb{\psi}_{\leq \ell}^{\top}\pmb {q}\pmb {u}\in \mathcal{v}_1\}$ ,  $\mathcal{v}_2' = \mathrm{span}\{\pmb {u}\in \mathbb{r}^d:$ $\pmb{\psi}_{\leq \ell}^{\top}\pmb {q}\pmb {u}\in \mathcal{v}_2\}$ . note that  $\pmb{\psi}_{\leq \ell}^{\top}\pmb{q}$  has full rank, so  $\dim (\mathcal{v}_1') = k$  and  $\dim (\mathcal{v}_2') = d - k + 1$ .

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lambda_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \psi_ {\leq \ell} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2} \psi_ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q}\right) = \max  _ {\mathcal {v}: \dim (\mathcal {v}) = k} \min  _ {\boldsymbol {0} \neq \boldsymbol {u} \in \mathcal {v}} \frac {\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \psi_ {\leq \ell} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2} \psi_ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {u}}{\| \boldsymbol {u} \| ^ {2}} \\ \geq \left(1 - \check {\sigma} _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1)\right) \min  _ {\boldsymbol {0} \neq \boldsymbol {u} \in \mathcal {v} _ {1} ^ {\prime}} \frac {\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {u}}{\| \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {u} \| ^ {2} / n} \\ \geq \left(1 - \check {o} _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1)\right) \min  _ {\mathbf {0} \neq \mathbf {x} \in \mathcal {v} _ {1}} \frac {\mathbf {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2} \mathbf {x}}{\| \mathbf {x} \| ^ {2} / n} \geq n \left(1 - \check {o} _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1)\right) \cdot \lambda_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

similarly,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lambda_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \psi_ {\leq \ell} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2} \psi_ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q}\right) = \min  _ {\mathcal {v}: \dim (\mathcal {v}) = d - k + 1} \max  _ {\boldsymbol {u} \in \mathcal {v}} \frac {\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \psi_ {\leq \ell} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2} \psi_ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {u}}{\| \boldsymbol {u} \| ^ {2}} \\ \leq \left(1 + \breve {o} _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1)\right) \max  _ {\boldsymbol {u} \in \mathcal {v} _ {2} ^ {\prime}} \frac {\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {u}}{\| \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {u} \| ^ {2} / n} \\ \leq \left(1 + \check {o} _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1)\right) \max  _ {\boldsymbol {x} \in \mathcal {v} _ {2}} \frac {\boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {x}}{\| \boldsymbol {x} \| ^ {2} / n} \leq n \left(1 + \check {o} _ {d, \mathbb {p}} (1)\right) \cdot \lambda_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\leq \ell} ^ {2}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

finally, we recall that  $b(d,k) = (1 + o_d(1))\cdot d^k /k!$ . this then leads to the claim (92).
