this lemma is a quantitative refinement of [gmmm21][prop. 3]. difference from the moment method employed in [gmmm21][prop. 3], the proof of this lemma uses a specific matrix concentration inequality, namely the matrix version of freedman's inequality for martingales, first established by  $\left[\mathrm{t}^{+}11\right]$  (see also [oli09]).

theorem e.1. consider a matrix-valued martingale  $\{\mathbf{y}_i:i = 0,1,\ldots \}$  with respect to the filtration  $(\mathcal{f}_i)_{i = 0}^{\infty}$ . the values of  $\mathbf{y}_i$  are symmetric matrices with dimension  $n$ . let  $\mathbf{z}_i = \mathbf{y}_i - \mathbf{y}_{i - 1}$  for all  $i\geq 1$ . assume that  $\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathbf{z}_i)\leq \overline{l}$  almost surely for all  $i\geq 1$ . then, for all  $t\geq 0$  and  $v\geq 0$

$$
\mathbb {p} \big (\exists k \geq 0: \lambda_ {\max} (\boldsymbol {y} _ {k}) \geq t a n d \| \mathbf {v} _ {k} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq v \big) \leq n \cdot \exp \left(- \frac {t ^ {2} / 2}{v + \overline {{l}} t / 3}\right)
$$

where  $\mathbf{v}_i$  is defined as  $\mathbf{v}_i = \sum_{j=1}^i \mathbb{e}[z_j^2 | \mathcal{f}_{i-1}]$ .

first, we fix  $k > \ell$  and treat it as a constant. we also suppress the dependence on  $k$  if there is no confusion. define a filtration  $(\mathcal{f}_i)_{i=0}^n$  and random matrices  $(z_i)_{i=1}^n$  as follows. we define  $\mathcal{f}_j$  to be the  $\sigma$ -algebra generated by  $x_1, \ldots, x_j$ ; particularly,  $\mathcal{f}_0$  is the trivial  $\sigma$ -algebra. we also define the truncated version of  $q$ .

$$
\overline {{q}} = \left(q _ {i j} \xi_ {i j} \mathbf {1} \{i \neq j \}\right) _ {i, j \leq n}, \quad \text {w h e r e} \xi_ {i j} = \mathbf {1} \{\left| \langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {j} \rangle \right| \leq \sqrt {d} \log d \} \tag {157}
$$

by a simple concentration result (lemma 22), we have, with very high probability,

$$
\max _ {i \neq j} | \langle \pmb {x} _ {i}, \pmb {x} _ {j} \rangle | \leq \sqrt {d} \log d.
$$

note  $q_{ii} = 1$  always holds. so  $\overline{\mathbf{q}} = \mathbf{q} - \mathbf{i}_n$  holds with very high probability. define

$$
\boldsymbol {z} _ {i} := \mathbb {e} [ \overline {{\boldsymbol {q}}} | \mathcal {f} _ {i} ] - \mathbb {e} [ \overline {{\boldsymbol {q}}} | \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} ].
$$

in other words,  $(\mathbb{e}[\overline{\boldsymbol{q}} |\mathcal{f}_i])_{i = 1}^n$  is a doob martingale with respect to the filtration  $(\mathcal{f}_i)_{i = 0}^n$ , and  $(z_{i})_{i = 1}^{n}$  is the resulting martingale difference sequence. note that trivially  $\overline{\boldsymbol{q}} = \mathbb{e}[\overline{\boldsymbol{q}} |\mathcal{f}_n]$ ,  $\mathbb{e}[\overline{\boldsymbol{q}} ] = \mathbb{e}[\overline{\boldsymbol{q}} |\mathcal{f}_0]$ . using this definition, we can express  $\overline{\boldsymbol{q}}$  as

$$
\overline {{\boldsymbol {q}}} = \mathbb {e} [ \overline {{\boldsymbol {q}}} ] + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \boldsymbol {z} _ {i}. \tag {158}
$$

by construction,  $\mathbb{e}[\pmb{q}] = \mathbf{i}_d$ . it is not difficult to show that  $\left\| \mathbb{e}[\overline{\pmb{q}}] \right\|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq c d^{-k}$ ; see lemma 25 below. crucially, we claim that the matrix  $\pmb{z}_i$  is a rank-2 matrix plus a small perturbation (namely, a matrix with very small operator norm). the perturbation term is due to the effect of truncation.

lemma 25. let  $k \geq \ell + 1$  be a constant. then we have decomposition

$$
\boldsymbol {z} _ {i} = \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {i}, \quad \text {w h e r e} (\boldsymbol {v} _ {i}) _ {j} = \overline {{q}} _ {i j} \mathbf {1} \{j <   i \}. \tag {159}
$$

here  $\pmb{\delta}_i\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times n}$  is certain matrix that satisfies  $\| \pmb {\delta}_i\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq cd^{-k}$  almost surely, and  $\pmb {v}_i$  satisfies  $\| \pmb {v}_i\| \leq c\sqrt{\frac{n(\log d)^{2k}}{d^k}}$  almost surely. in addition,  $\| \mathbb{e}[\overline{\mathbf{q}} ]\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq cd^{-k}$

proof of lemma 25. in this proof, we will use the identities

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ q \left(\langle \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\prime} \rangle\right) \right] = 0, \quad \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ q \left(\langle \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\prime} \rangle\right) ^ {2} \right] = b (d, k) ^ {- 1}, \tag {160}
$$

where  $\pmb{x},\pmb{x}^{\prime}\sim_{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}\mathrm{unif}(\mathbb{s}^{d - 1}(\sqrt{d}))$ . these identities can be derived from (41).

let  $j, m \in [n]$  be indices. since  $z_{i}$  is symmetric, there is no loss of generality in only considering that  $j < m$  (note  $(z_{i})_{jj} = 0$  if  $j = m$ ). if  $m < i$ , then  $\overline{q}_{jm} \in \mathcal{f}_{i-1}$  so  $[z_{i}]_{jm} = 0$ . next we consider  $m > i$ . observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x} _ {m}} \left[ q (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {j}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {m} \rangle) \mathbf {1} \left\{\left| \langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {j}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {m} \rangle \right| \leq c _ {1} \sqrt {d} \log d \right\} \right] \right| \\ \stackrel {(i)} {=} \left| \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x} _ {m}} \left[ q (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {j}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {m} \rangle) \mathbf {1} \left\{\left| \langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {j}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {m} \rangle \right| > c _ {1} \sqrt {d} \log d \right\} \right] \right| \\ \stackrel {(i i)} {\leq} \left\{\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x} _ {m}} \left[ q \left(\left\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {j}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {m} \right\rangle\right) ^ {2} \right] \right\} ^ {1 / 2} \cdot \left\{\mathbb {p} _ {\boldsymbol {x} _ {m}} \left(\left| \left\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {j}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {m} \right\rangle \right| > c _ {1} \sqrt {d} \log d\right) \right\} ^ {1 / 2} \\ \stackrel {(i i i)} {\leq} b (d, k) ^ {- 1 / 2} \cdot o _ {d} (d ^ {- 2 k}) = o _ {d} (d ^ {- 2 k}), \\ \end{array}
$$

where in (i) we used (160), in (ii) we used the cauchy-schwarz inequality, and in (iii) we used (160) and lemma 22. this implies

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ q _ {j m} \xi_ {j m} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {i} \right] = o _ {d} \left(d ^ {- 2 k}\right), \quad \mathbb {e} \left[ q _ {j m} \xi_ {j m} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} \right] = o _ {d} \left(d ^ {- 2 k}\right). \tag {161}
$$

together, these bounds imply  $|[z_i]_{jm}| = o_d(d^{-2k})$ . finally, consider  $m = i$ . a similar argument gives

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ q _ {j m} \xi_ {j m} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {i} \right] = q _ {j m} \xi_ {j m} = \overline {{q}} _ {j m}, \qquad \mathbb {e} \left[ q _ {j m} \xi_ {j m} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} \right] = o _ {d} (d ^ {- 2 k}).
$$

combining the three cases, we derive the expression (159). the residual satisfies

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq n \left\| \boldsymbol {\delta} \right\| _ {\max } = n \cdot o _ {d} (d ^ {- 2 k}) \leq o _ {d} (d ^ {- k})
$$

where the last inequality is due to  $d^{k} \geq n$ . note that (161) also implies

$$
\| \mathbb {e} [ \overline {{\boldsymbol {q}}} ] \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq n \cdot \max  _ {j m} \left| \mathbb {e} \left[ q _ {j m} \xi_ {j, m} \right] \right| \leq o _ {d} (n d ^ {- 2 k}) \leq o _ {d} (d ^ {- k}).
$$

by the property (44) of the gegenbauer polynomials, we know that the coefficients of  $b(d,k)^{1/2}q(\cdot)$  are of constant order, so we have the deterministic bound

$$
\left| b (d, k) ^ {1 / 2} q \left(\left\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {j} \right\rangle\right) \right| \xi_ {i j} \leq \left(1 + o _ {d} (1)\right) \cdot h _ {k} \left(\frac {\left\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} , \boldsymbol {x} _ {j} \right\rangle}{\sqrt {d}}\right) \xi_ {i j} \leq o _ {d} \left(\left(\log d\right) ^ {k}\right).
$$

since  $b(d,k) = (1 + o_d(1))\cdot d^k /k!$ , this gives  $\max_{ij}|\overline{q}_{ij}|\leq c\sqrt{(\log d)^k / d^k}$  and thus

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \right\| \leq \sqrt {i} \cdot \max  _ {j <   i} | \overline {{q}} _ {i j} | \leq c \sqrt {\frac {n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}}}.
$$

this completes the proof.

before applying the matrix concentration inequality to the sum  $\sum_{i=1}^{n} z_i$ , let us define

$$
l := \max  _ {i \leq n} \| \boldsymbol {z} _ {i} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}, \quad v := \left\| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {z} _ {i} ^ {2} | \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}}.
$$

using lemma 25, we obtain deterministic bounds on  $l$  and  $v$ , as stated below.

lemma 26. the following holds almost surely.

$$
\max  _ {i \leq n} \| z _ {i} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq c \sqrt {\frac {n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}}}, \tag {162}
$$

$$
\left\| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {z} _ {i} ^ {2} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} \right] \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq c \frac {n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}} + \frac {n}{b (d , k)} \| \boldsymbol {q} - \mathbf {i} _ {n} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}. \tag {163}
$$

proof of lemma 26. the first inequality follows directly from lemma 25:

$$
\max  _ {i \leq n} \| \boldsymbol {z} _ {i} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq 2 \max  _ {i \leq n} \| \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \| + \max  _ {i \leq n} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {i} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq c \sqrt {\frac {n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}}} + c d ^ {- k} \leq c \sqrt {\frac {n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}}}.
$$

to prove the second inequality, we use the decomposition (159).

$$
\mathbb {e} \big [ \boldsymbol {z} _ {i} ^ {2} | \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} \big ] = \mathbb {e} \big [ \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} ^ {\top} | \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} \big ] + \mathbb {e} \big [ \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} ^ {\top} \| \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \| ^ {2} | \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} \big ] + \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\delta}} _ {i}
$$

where  $\widetilde{\delta}_i\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times n}$  is given by

$$
\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\delta}} _ {i} = \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {i} + \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {i} + \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {i} \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {i} \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {i} ^ {2} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} \right].
$$

note that we used  $\pmb{e}_i^\top \pmb{v}_i = 0$  in the above calculation. using the deterministic bounds on  $\| \pmb{v}_i\|$  and  $\| \pmb{\delta}_i\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ , we get

$$
\| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\delta}} _ {i} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq 4 \| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\delta}} _ {i} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \cdot \| \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \| + \| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\delta}} _ {i} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \leq \frac {c}{d ^ {k}} \sqrt {\frac {n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}}} + \frac {c}{d ^ {2 k}}.
$$

we also note that  $\sum_{i\leq n}\mathbb{e}\big[e_i e_i^\top \| \pmb {v}_i\| ^2\big|\mathcal{f}_{i - 1}\big]$  is a diagonal matrix with its diagonal entries bounded by  $\max_{i\leq n}\| \pmb {v}_i\|^2$ . thus, we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \sum_ {i \leq n} \mathbb {e} \big [ z _ {i} ^ {2} | \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} \big ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq n \max _ {i \leq n} \left\| \mathbb {e} \big [ \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} ^ {\top} | \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} \big ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} + \max _ {i \leq n} \| \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \| ^ {2} + n \max _ {i \leq n} \| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\delta}} _ {i} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \\ \leq n \max _ {i \leq n} \left\| \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} ^ {\top} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} \right] \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} + \frac {c n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}} + \frac {c n}{d ^ {k}} \sqrt {\frac {n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}}} + \frac {c n}{d ^ {2 k}} \\ \leq n \max  _ {i \leq n} \left\| \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} ^ {\top} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} \right] \right\| _ {\text {o p}} + \frac {c n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}} \tag {164} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality is because

$$
\frac {n}{d ^ {k}} \sqrt {\frac {n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}}} \leq \sqrt {\frac {n}{d ^ {k}}} \cdot \sqrt {\frac {n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}}} \leq \frac {n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}}, \quad \frac {n}{d ^ {2 k}} \leq \frac {n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}},
$$

due to the assumption  $d^k \geq n$ . to further simplify (164), we note that

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} ^ {\top} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} \right] _ {j m} = 0, \quad \text {i f} m \geq i \text {o r} j \geq i.
$$

now we consider the case where  $m < i$  and  $j < i$ .

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} ^ {\top} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} \right] _ {j m} = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}} \left[ q (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {j} \rangle) q (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {m} \rangle) \xi_ {i j} \xi_ {i m} \right] \\ = \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}} \left[ q \left(\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {j} \rangle\right) q \left(\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {m} \rangle\right) \right] - \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}} \left[ q \left(\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {j} \rangle\right) q \left(\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {m} \rangle\right) \left(1 - \xi_ {i j} \xi_ {i m}\right) \right] \\ = \frac {1}{b (d , k)} q (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {j}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {m} \rangle) - \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}} \left[ q _ {i j} q _ {i m} (1 - \xi_ {i j} \xi_ {i m}) \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

where we used the identity (40) in the last equality. we write  $1 - \xi_{ij}\xi_{im} = (1 - \xi_{ij})\xi_{im} + 1 - \xi_{im}$  and use the cauchy-schwarz inequality to derive

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}} \left[ q _ {i j} q _ {i m} (1 - \xi_ {i j} \xi_ {i m}) \right] \right| \leq \left\{\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}} \left[ q _ {i j} ^ {2} q _ {i m} ^ {2} \right] \right\} ^ {1 / 2} \cdot \left\{\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}} \left[ (1 - \xi_ {i j}) ^ {2} \xi_ {i m} ^ {2} \right] \right\} ^ {1 / 2} \\ + \left\{\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}} \left[ q _ {i j} ^ {2} q _ {i m} ^ {2} \right] \right\} ^ {1 / 2} \cdot \left\{\mathbb {e} _ {\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}} \left[ (1 - \xi_ {i m}) ^ {2} \right] \right\} ^ {1 / 2} \\ \stackrel {(i)} {\leq} \left[ \mathbb {p} _ {\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}} (\xi_ {i j} = 0) \right] ^ {1 / 2} + \left[ \mathbb {p} _ {\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}} (\xi_ {i m} = 0) \right] ^ {1 / 2} \\ \stackrel {(i i)} {\leq} o _ {d} (d ^ {- 2 k}). \\ \end{array}
$$

where in  $(i)$  we used the inequality  $|q_{ij}|\leq 1$ , and in  $(ii)$  we used lemma 22. therefore,

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} ^ {\top} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} \right] \preceq \frac {1}{b (d , k)} \boldsymbol {q} + o _ {d} (n d ^ {- 2 k}) \cdot \mathbf {i} _ {n}.
$$

thus, we obtain

$$
\left\| \sum_ {i \leq n} \mathbb {e} \left[ \boldsymbol {z} _ {i} ^ {2} | \mathcal {f} _ {i - 1} \right] \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {n}{b (d , k)} \| \boldsymbol {q} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} + \frac {c n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}} \leq \frac {n}{b (d , k)} \| \boldsymbol {q} - \mathbf {i} _ {n} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} + \frac {c n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}}.
$$

this completes the proof.

once this lemma is established, we apply theorem e.1 to the martingales  $\mathbf{y}_m \coloneqq \sum_{i \leq m} \mathbf{z}_i$  and  $\mathbf{y}_m' \coloneqq \sum_{i \leq m} (-\mathbf{z}_i)$  (where we simply set  $\mathbf{z}_{n+i} = \mathbf{0}$  for  $i \geq 1$ ), and we obtain the following result. for any  $t \geq 0$  and  $v > 0$ ,

$$
\mathbb {p} \left(\left\| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} z _ {i} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \geq t, \text {a n d} v \leq v\right) \leq 2 n \cdot \exp \left(- \frac {t ^ {2} / 2}{v + \bar {l} t / 3}\right),
$$

where  $\overline{l} = c\sqrt{\frac{n(\log d)^{2k}}{d^k}}$  is the upper bound on  $l$  in (162). this inequality implies a probability tail bound on  $\| \sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{z}_i\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ .

$$
\mathbb {p} \left(\left\| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \boldsymbol {z} _ {i} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \geq t\right) \leq 2 n \cdot \exp \left(- \frac {t ^ {2} / 2}{v + \bar {l} t / 3}\right) + \mathbb {p} (v > v). \tag {165}
$$

by taking  $v$  slightly larger the "typical" values of  $v$ , we can make the probability  $\mathbb{p}\big(v > v\big)$  very small, which can be bounded by a tail probability of  $\| q\|_{\mathrm{op}}$  according to (163). this leads to a recursive inequality between tail probabilities. by abuse of notations, in the next lemma we assume that constant  $c\geq 1$  is chosen to be no smaller than those that appeared in lemma 25 and 26 (so that we can invoke both results).

lemma 27. let the constant  $c \geq 1$  be no smaller than those in lemma 25 and 26. suppose that

$$
t \geq \max  \left\{8 c \sqrt {\frac {n (\log d) ^ {2 k + 4}}{d ^ {k}}}, 1 2 8 \frac {n (\log d) ^ {2}}{b (d , k)} \right\}. \tag {166}
$$

then, by setting  $\overline{l} = c\sqrt{\frac{n(\log d)^{2k}}{d^k}}$  and  $v = \frac{t^2}{32(\log d)^2}$ , we have

$$
\mathbb {p} \left(\left\| \boldsymbol {q} - \mathbf {i} _ {d} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \geq t\right) \leq \mathbb {p} \left(\left\| \boldsymbol {q} - \mathbf {i} _ {d} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \geq 2 t\right) + c ^ {\prime} \exp \left(- (\log d) ^ {2}\right) + \mathbb {p} \left(\overline {{\boldsymbol {q}}} \neq \boldsymbol {q} - \mathbf {i} _ {n}\right). \tag {167}
$$

as a consequence, by iterating the choice of  $t$  at most  $o_{d}(\log d)$  times, we obtain that  $\left\| \mathbf{q} - \mathbf{i}_d\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq t$  holds with very high probability.

proof of lemma 27. if  $t$  satisfies (166), then we derive

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {p} \big (\| q - \mathbf {i} _ {n} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} > t \big) \leq \mathbb {p} \big (\| \overline {{q}} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} > t \big) + \mathbb {p} \big (\overline {{q}} \neq q - \mathbf {i} _ {n} \big) \\ \leq \mathbb {p} \left(\left\| \sum_ {i \leq n} \boldsymbol {z} _ {i} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} + \left\| \mathbb {e} [ \overline {{\boldsymbol {q}}} ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} > t\right) + \mathbb {p} \left(\overline {{\boldsymbol {q}}} \neq \boldsymbol {q} - \mathbf {i} _ {n}\right) \\ \stackrel {(i)} {\leq} \mathbb {p} \left(\left\| \sum_ {i \leq n} \boldsymbol {z} _ {i} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} > \frac {t}{2}\right) + \mathbb {p} \left(\overline {{\boldsymbol {q}}} \neq \boldsymbol {q} - \mathbf {i} _ {n}\right) \\ \leq \mathbb {p} (v > v) + n \exp \left(- \frac {t ^ {2} / 8}{v + \bar {l} t / 6}\right) + \mathbb {p} (\overline {{q}} \neq q - i _ {n}) \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $(i)$  is because  $\left\| \mathbb{e}[\overline{\mathbf{q}}]\right\|_{\mathrm{op}} < t / 2$ . by lemma 26 eq. (163), we get

$$
\mathbb {p} (v > v) \leq \mathbb {p} \big (c \frac {n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}} + \frac {n}{b (d , k)} \| \boldsymbol {q} - \mathbf {i} _ {n} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} > \frac {t ^ {2}}{3 2 (\log d) ^ {2}} \big).
$$

by the assumption on  $t$ , we have

$$
c \frac {n (\log d) ^ {2 k}}{d ^ {k}} \leq \frac {t ^ {2}}{6 4 (\log d) ^ {2}}, \quad \frac {2 t n}{b (d , k)} \leq \frac {t ^ {2}}{6 4 (\log d) ^ {2}}.
$$

this leads to  $\mathbb{p}(v > v)\leq \mathbb{p}(\| \pmb {q} - \mathbf{i}_n\|_{\mathrm{op}} > 2t)$ . also,  $\overline{l} t / 6\leq t^2 /(32(\log d)^2)$ , so

$$
\begin{array}{l} n \exp \big (- \frac {t ^ {2} / 8}{v + \bar {l} t / 6} \big) \leq n \exp \big (- \frac {t ^ {2} / 8}{v + t ^ {2} / (3 2 (\log d) ^ {2})} \big) \\ = n \exp \left(- 2 (\log d) ^ {2}\right) \leq d ^ {k} \cdot \exp \left(- 2 (\log d) ^ {2}\right) \\ \leq c ^ {\prime} \exp \big (- (\log d) ^ {2} \big). \\ \end{array}
$$

this proves the inequality (167). we note that there is a naive deterministic bound

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {q} - \mathbf {i} _ {n} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq n \left\| \boldsymbol {q} - \mathbf {i} _ {n} \right\| _ {\max } \leq 2 n.
$$

we apply (167) in which  $t$  is set to one of  $\{2t, 4t, \dots, 2^s t\}$  where  $s \geq k \log d / \log 2$ . summing these inequalities and using the naive deterministic bound, we obtain

$$
\mathbb {p} \left(\left\| \boldsymbol {q} - \mathbf {i} _ {n} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} > t\right) \leq o _ {d} (\log d) \cdot \big (\exp \big (- (\log d) ^ {2} \big) + \mathbb {p} \big (\overline {{\boldsymbol {q}}} \neq \boldsymbol {q} - \mathbf {i} _ {n} \big) \big).
$$

since  $\overline{\mathbf{q}} = \mathbf{q} - \mathbf{i}_n$  happens with high probability, the above inequality implies that  $\| \mathbf{q} - \mathbf{i}_n\|_{\mathrm{op}}$  also happens with very high probability.

finally, we are in a position to prove proposition e.1. note that lemma 27 already gives the right bound on  $\| q_k - \mathbf{i}_n\|_{\mathrm{op}}$  for a constant  $k$ . for very large  $k$ , we resort to a different approach.

proof of proposition e.1. let  $s \geq 1$  be a constant integer. our goal is to prove

$$
d ^ {s} \cdot \mathbb {p} \left(\sup  _ {k > \ell} \left\| \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} - \mathbf {i} _ {d} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq c ^ {\prime} \sqrt {\frac {n (\log d) ^ {c ^ {\prime}}}{d ^ {\ell + 1}}}\right) = o _ {d} (1) \tag {168}
$$

for certain sufficiently large constant  $c'$ .

consider  $k \geq k_0 := 4\ell + s + 7$ . since  $\mathbb{e}\bigl[q_k(\langle \pmb{x}_i, \pmb{x}_j \rangle)^2\bigr] = b(d, k)^{-1}$ , by markov's inequality, for any  $t > 0$ , we have

$$
\mathbb {p} \left(\left| q _ {k} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {j} \rangle) \right| > d ^ {- t}\right) \leq \frac {d ^ {2 t}}{b (d , k)}.
$$

thus, by the union bound,

$$
\mathbb {p} \big (\big \| \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} - \mathbf {i} _ {d} \big \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} > n d ^ {- t} \big) \leq \mathbb {p} \big (n \max _ {i \neq j} \big | q _ {k} (\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {j} \rangle) \big | > n d ^ {- t} \big) \leq \frac {n ^ {2} d ^ {2 t}}{b (d , k)}.
$$

we choose  $t = \ell + 2$ , so that

$$
n d ^ {- t} <   \frac {n}{d ^ {\ell + 1}} <   c \sqrt {\frac {n (\log d) ^ {c}}{d ^ {\ell + 1}}}, \qquad \sum_ {k > k _ {0}} \frac {n ^ {2} d ^ {2 t}}{b (d , k)} \leq d ^ {2 \ell + 2 + t} \sum_ {k > k _ {0}} \frac {1}{b (d , k)} \leq c d ^ {- s - 1}
$$

where we used the inequality  $\sum_{k > k_0} \frac{1}{b(d,k)} \leq c d^{-k_0}$ . so taking the union bound, we get

$$
\mathbb {p} \left(\sup  _ {k \geq k _ {0}} \left\| \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} - \mathbf {i} _ {d} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} > c \sqrt {\frac {n (\log d) ^ {c}}{d ^ {\ell + 1}}}\right) \leq \sum_ {k > k _ {0}} \frac {n ^ {2} d ^ {t}}{b (d , k)} \leq c d ^ {- s - 1}. \tag {169}
$$

further, we apply lemma 27 with  $k$  set to  $\{\ell +1,\ell +2,\ldots ,k_0 - 1\}$ , and we find

$$
d ^ {s} \cdot \mathbb {p} \left(\sup  _ {\ell <   k <   k _ {0}} \left\| \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} - \mathbf {i} _ {d} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} > c \sqrt {\frac {n (\log d) ^ {c}}{d ^ {\ell + 1}}}\right) = o _ {d} (1). \tag {170}
$$

combining (169) with (170) yields the desired goal (168).

# proof for network capacity upper bound

proof of theorem 3.1. we denote by  $\pmb{\theta} = (\pmb{b},\mathbf{w})$  the collection of neural network parameters and by  $f_{\pmb{\theta}}$  the associated function.

let  $m = m_d > 1$  be a positive integer to be specified later. we define the discretization set  $\mathcal{d}_m = \{0, \pm \frac{1}{m}, \pm \frac{2}{m}, \pm \frac{3}{m}, \ldots\}$  and

$$
\theta_ {l} := \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta}: b _ {\ell} \in \mathcal {d} _ {m}, \sqrt {d} w _ {\ell k} \in \mathcal {d} _ {m}, \forall \ell \in [ n ], \forall k \in [ d ] \right\}, \tag {171}
$$

$$
\theta_ {l, m} := \theta_ {l} \cap \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta}: b _ {\ell} \in \mathcal {d} _ {m}, \sqrt {d} w _ {\ell k} \in \mathcal {d} _ {m}, \forall \ell \in [ n ], \forall k \in [ d ] \right\}. \tag {172}
$$

each  $f\in \mathcal{f}_{\mathbb{n}\mathbb{n}}^{n,l}$  is associated with an  $\pmb {\theta}\in \theta_l$

(1) lower bound on discretized parameter space. we make the following claim. if with probability at least  $\eta_1 / 2$ , there exists certain  $\overline{\theta} \in \theta_{l,m}$  such that at least  $(1 / 2 + \eta_2 / 2)n$  examples are correctly classified, i.e.,

$$
n ^ {- 1} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbf {1} \left\{y _ {i} f _ {\overline {{\theta}}} \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}\right) > 0 \right\} \geq \frac {1}{2} + \frac {\eta_ {2}}{2}, \tag {173}
$$

then we have  $nd = \omega_d\left(\frac{n}{\log(lm)}\right)$ .

to prove this claim, we treat the input  $\pmb{x}_i$  as deterministic. we derive

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\boldsymbol {y}} \left(\exists \bar {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \in \theta_ {l, m} \text {s u c h t h a t} | \{i: \operatorname {s i g n} \left(f _ {\bar {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}\right)\right) = y _ {i} \} | \geq (1 / 2 + \eta_ {2} / 2) n\right) \tag {174}
$$

$$
\stackrel {(i)} {\leq} \left| \theta_ {l, m} \right| \cdot \mathbb {p} _ {\boldsymbol {y}} \big (\left| \left\{i: \operatorname {s i g n} \left(f _ {\overline {{\boldsymbol {\theta}}}} \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}\right)\right) = y _ {i} \right\} \right| \geq (1 / 2 + \eta_ {2} / 2) n \big)
$$

$$
\stackrel {(i i)} {\leq} o _ {d} (1) \cdot \left[ \sqrt {2 \pi e} (4 m l + 1) \right] ^ {n d + n} \cdot \mathbb {p} _ {\boldsymbol {y}} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \xi_ {i} \geq (1 / 2 + \eta_ {2} / 2) n\right)
$$

where  $\xi_{i} = 1\{\mathrm{sign}(f_{\overline{\theta}}(\pmb{x}_{i})) = y_{i}\}$  denotes a bernoulli random variable with mean  $1 / 2$ . here, (i) used the union bound and (ii) used the following bound on the cardinality of  $\theta_{l,m}$

$$
\left| \theta_ {l, m} \right| = o _ {d} (1) \cdot \left[ \sqrt {2 \pi e} (4 m l + 1) \right] ^ {n d + n},
$$

which is proved via the covering number argument (see lemma 28). by hoeffding's inequality, we have

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\boldsymbol {y}} \big (\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \xi_ {i} \geq (1 / 2 + \eta_ {2} / 2) n \big) \leq \exp \big (- 2 n \eta_ {2} ^ {2} \big).
$$

since we assume that the probability of the event in (174) is at least  $\eta_{1}$ , we take the logarithm and deduce

$$
\log \left(\eta_ {1} / 2\right) \leq o _ {d} (1) \cdot n (d + 1) \log (4 l m + 1) - n \eta_ {2} ^ {2} / 2, \quad \text {o r s i m p l y}
$$

$$
n = o _ {d} \big (n d \log (l m + 1) \big).
$$

it then follows that  $nd = o_{d}\left(\frac{n}{\log(lm + 1)}\right)$ .

(2) projecting  $\theta$  onto the discretized set. for any  $z\in \mathbb{r}$ , let  $\tau_{1}(z)$  and  $\tau_{2}(z)$  be the elements in  $\mathcal{d}_m$  such that the distances to  $z$  are the smallest one and the second smallest one (break ties in an arbitrary way). for a given  $z$ , define a random variable  $\xi (z)\in \{\tau_1(z),\tau_2(z)\}$  by

$$
\xi (z) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \tau_ {1} (z), & \text {w i t h p r o b a b i l i t y} \frac {| z - \tau_ {2} (z) |}{| z - \tau_ {1} (z) | + | z - \tau_ {2} (z) |}; \\ \tau_ {2} (z), & \text {w i t h p r o b a b i l i t y} \frac {| z - \tau_ {1} (z) |}{| z - \tau_ {1} (z) | + | z - \tau_ {2} (z) |}. \end{array} \right.
$$

this definition ensures that  $\mathbb{e}\xi (z) = z$ . indeed,  $z - \tau_{1}(z)$  and  $z - \tau_{2}(z)$  have the opposite signs, so

$$
z - \mathbb {e} \xi (z) = \frac {(z - \tau_ {1} (z)) | z - \tau_ {2} (z) |}{| z - \tau_ {1} (z) | + | z - \tau_ {2} (z) |} + \frac {(z - \tau_ {2} (z)) | z - \tau_ {1} (z) |}{| z - \tau_ {1} (z) | + | z - \tau_ {2} (z) |} = 0.
$$

denote  $\widetilde{b}_{\ell} = \xi(b_{\ell})$  and  $\widetilde{w}_{\ell k} = d^{-1/2}\xi(d^{1/2}w_{\ell k})$  for all  $\ell$  and  $k$  (they are independent random variables), and  $\widetilde{\pmb{\theta}} = (\widetilde{\pmb{b}}, \widetilde{\mathbf{w}})$ . then  $\widetilde{\pmb{\theta}}$  is a random projection of  $\pmb{\theta} \in \theta_l$  onto the discretized set  $\theta_{l,m}$ .

(3) bounding the approximation error. by assumption, with probability greater than  $\eta_{1}$ , there exists  $\pmb{\theta} \in \theta_{l}$  (which depends on the data  $(\pmb{x}_i)_{i \leq 1}$  and  $(y_i)_{i \leq n}$ ) such that it gives more than  $\delta$  margin on at least  $(1/2 + \eta_2)n$  examples. denote by  $\mathcal{i} \coloneqq \mathcal{i}(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{y}) \subset [n]$  the set of indices  $i$  such that  $y_i f_\pmb{\theta}(\pmb{x}_i) > \delta$ . then, the assumption is equivalent to  $|\mathcal{i}| \geq (1/2 + \eta_2)n$  with probability great than  $\eta_1$ .

we claim

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\mathbf {x}, \boldsymbol {y}, \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} \left(\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbf {1} \left\{\left| f _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}\right) - f _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}\right) \right| <   \delta \right\} \geq 1 - \frac {\eta_ {2}}{2}\right) \geq 1 - \frac {\eta_ {1}}{2}. \tag {175}
$$

here we emphasize that  $\mathbb{p}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}$  is the probability measure over all random variables (namely the data  $\mathbf{x}$ ,  $\mathbf{y}$  and also  $\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$ ). once this claim is proved, we can immediately prove the desired result. in fact, for a given  $\overline{\boldsymbol{\theta}} \in \theta_{l,m}$ , let us define the indicator variable

$$
i _ {\overline {{\boldsymbol {\theta}}}} = \mathbf {1} \left\{\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbf {1} \left\{| f _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}) - f _ {\overline {{\boldsymbol {\theta}}}} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}) | <   \delta \right\} \geq 1 - \frac {\eta_ {2}}{2} \right\}
$$

we observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbf {x}, \boldsymbol {y}, \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} [ i _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} ] = \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbf {x}, \boldsymbol {y}} \mathbb {e} _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} | \mathbf {x}, \boldsymbol {y}} [ i _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} ] \stackrel {(i)} {\leq} \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbf {x}, \boldsymbol {y}} \left[ \max  _ {\overline {{\boldsymbol {\theta}}} \in \theta_ {l, m}} [ i _ {\overline {{\boldsymbol {\theta}}}} ] \right] \stackrel {(i i)} {=} \mathbb {p} _ {\mathbf {x}, \boldsymbol {y}} \left[ \max  _ {\overline {{\boldsymbol {\theta}}} \in \theta_ {l, m}} [ i _ {\overline {{\boldsymbol {\theta}}}} ] = 1 \right] \\ = \mathbb {p} _ {\mathbf {x}, \mathbf {y}} \left[ \exists \bar {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \in \theta_ {l, m} \text {s u c h t h a t} i _ {\bar {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} = 1 \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

where (i) is because the mean is no larger than the maximum and (ii) is because  $\max_{\overline{\theta} \in \theta_{l,m}} [i_{\overline{\theta}}]$  takes value 0 or 1. combining this with (175), we deduce that with probability at least  $1 - \eta_1 / 2$ , there exists an  $\overline{\theta} \in \theta_{l,m}$  such that  $|f_{\pmb{\theta}}(\pmb{x}_i) - f_{\overline{\pmb{\theta}}}(\pmb{x}_i)| < \delta$  for all  $i \in \mathcal{i}'$  where  $\mathcal{i}' \subset [n]$  satisfies  $|\mathcal{i}'| \geq (1 - \eta_2 / 2)n$ .

we use the fact that  $\mathbb{p}(\mathcal{a}_1\cap \mathcal{a}_2)\geq \mathbb{p}(\mathcal{a}_1) + \mathcal{p}(\mathcal{a}_2) - 1$  for arbitrary events  $\mathcal{a}_1,\mathcal{a}_2$  to deduce that with probability at least  $\eta_1 / 2$ , there exists an  $\overline{\theta}\in \theta_{l,m}$  such that  $|f_{\pmb{\theta}}(\pmb{x}_i) - f_{\overline{\pmb{\theta}}}(\pmb{x}_i)| < \delta$  for all  $i\in \mathcal{i}'$ , and in the meantime  $|\mathcal{i}|\geq (1 / 2 + \eta_2)n$ . by the triangle inequality, for every  $i\in \mathcal{i}\cap \mathcal{i}'$

$$
y _ {i} f _ {\overline {{\boldsymbol {\theta}}}} \geq y _ {i} f _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} - \left| y _ {i} f _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}) - y _ {i} f _ {\overline {{\boldsymbol {\theta}}}} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}) \right| = y _ {i} f _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} - \left| f _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}) - f _ {\overline {{\boldsymbol {\theta}}}} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}) \right| > 0.
$$

note that  $|\mathcal{i} \cap \mathcal{i}'| \geq |\mathcal{i}| + |\mathcal{i}'| - n \geq (1/2 + \eta_2/2)n$ . therefore, the assumption of the discretized case, namely (1), is satisfied; and hence we obtain the desired lower bound.

below we prove the claim (175). denote  $h_{i\ell} = \sigma (\langle \pmb{w}_{\ell},\pmb{x}_{i}\rangle)$  and  $\delta h_{i\ell} = \sigma (\langle \pmb{w}_{\ell},\pmb{x}_{i}\rangle) - \sigma (\langle \widetilde{\pmb{w}}_{\ell},\pmb{x}_{i}\rangle)$ . by the triangle inequality,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| f _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}\right) - f _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}\right) \right| \leq \frac {1}{n} \left| \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} \left(b _ {\ell} - \widetilde {b} _ {\ell}\right) \sigma \left(\left\langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \right\rangle\right) \right| + \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} \left| \widetilde {b} _ {\ell} \left[ \sigma \left(\left\langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \right\rangle\right) - \sigma \left(\left\langle \widetilde {\boldsymbol {w}} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \right\rangle\right) \right] \right| \\ \leq \frac {1}{n} \left| \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} \left(b _ {\ell} - \widetilde {b} _ {\ell}\right) h _ {i \ell} \right| + \frac {l}{n} \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} | \delta h _ {i \ell} |, \\ \stackrel {(i)} {\leq} \frac {| \sigma (0) |}{n} \big | \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} (b _ {\ell} - \widetilde {b} _ {\ell}) \big | + \frac {1}{n} \big | \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} (b _ {\ell} - \widetilde {b} _ {\ell}) (h _ {i \ell} - \sigma (0)) \big | + \frac {l}{\sqrt {n}} \big (\sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} | \delta h _ {i \ell} | ^ {2} \big) ^ {1 / 2}, \\ =: t _ {i, 1} + t _ {i, 2} + t _ {i, 3}, \tag {176} \\ \end{array}
$$

where we used cauchy-schwarz inequality in (i).

i) bounding  $t_{i,1}$ . conditioning on  $\mathbf{x}$  and  $\pmb{y}$ , the random variable  $b_{\ell} - \widetilde{b}_{\ell}$  is independent across  $\ell$ , has zero mean, and satisfies  $|b_{\ell} - \widetilde{b}_{\ell}| \leq 1 / m$ . so we can use hoeffding's inequality to control the first term  $t_{1}$  in (176).

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} \big (\frac {| \sigma (0) |}{n} \big | \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} (b _ {\ell} - \widetilde {b} _ {\ell}) \big | > \frac {| \sigma (0) | t}{m} \big) \leq 2 e ^ {- n t ^ {2} / 2} \leq 2 e ^ {- t ^ {2} / 2}.
$$

taking  $t = \sqrt{2\log(16 / \eta_1)}$  yields the bound

$$
t _ {i, 1} \leq \frac {c _ {0} \sqrt {2 \log (1 6 / \eta_ {1})}}{m}
$$

with probability at least  $1 - \eta_1 / 8$ , where we used the assumption that  $|\sigma (0)|\leq c_0$

ii) bounding  $t_{i,2}$ . by assumption,  $\sigma$  has weak derivatives, so we have

$$
\sigma (\langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \rangle) - \sigma (0) = \langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \rangle \int_ {0} ^ {1} \sigma^ {\prime} (t \langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \rangle) d t
$$

since  $\max_{i\in [n]}\| \pmb {x}_i\| _2\leq 2\sqrt{d}$  with probability at least  $1 - 2ne^{-cd}$ , we have  $|\langle \pmb {w}_{\ell},\pmb {x}_i\rangle |\leq \| \pmb {w}_{\ell}\| _2\cdot \| \pmb {x}_i\| _2\leq$ $2l\sqrt{d}$ . thus

$$
| \sigma (\langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \rangle) - \sigma (0) | \leq \max  _ {| z | \leq 2 l \sqrt {d}} | \sigma^ {\prime} (z) | \cdot | \langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \rangle |.
$$

recall the assumption 3.2 on the activation function  $|\sigma'(z)| \leq b(1 + |z|)^b$ . we have  $\max_{|z| \leq 2l\sqrt{d}} |\sigma'(z)| \leq b[1 + (2l\sqrt{d})^b] \leq 2b(2l\sqrt{d})^b$ . therefore,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} | \sigma (\langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \rangle) - \sigma (0) | ^ {2} \leq 4 b ^ {2} (2 l \sqrt {d}) ^ {2 b} \cdot \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} | \langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \rangle | ^ {2} \\ = 4 b ^ {2} (2 l \sqrt {d}) ^ {2 b} \cdot \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} \| \mathbf {x} \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell} \| ^ {2} \\ \leq 4 b ^ {2} (2 l \sqrt {d}) ^ {2 b} \cdot \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} \| \mathbf {x} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell} \| ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

by standard random matrix theory [ver10, cor. 5.35],  $\| \mathbf{x}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2\sqrt{n} +\sqrt{d}\leq 3\sqrt{n}$  with probability at least  $1 - 2e^{-n^2 /2}$ . also,  $\| \pmb {w}_\ell \| ^2\leq l^2$ . so we get

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} | \sigma (\langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \rangle) - \sigma (0) | ^ {2} \leq 3 6 b ^ {2} l ^ {2} (2 l \sqrt {d}) ^ {2 b} n n. \tag {177}
$$

let  $\mathcal{i}_1\coloneqq \mathcal{i}_1(\mathbf{x},\pmb {y})\subset [n]$  be the set of  $i\in [n]$  such that

$$
\sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} \left| h _ {i \ell} - \sigma (0) \right| ^ {2} \leq 3 2 (\eta_ {1} \eta_ {2}) ^ {- 1} \times 3 6 b ^ {2} l ^ {2} (2 l \sqrt {d}) ^ {2 b} n.
$$

recall the definition  $h_{i\ell} = \sigma(\langle \pmb{w}_{\ell}, \pmb{x}_i \rangle)$ . then, with probability at least  $1 - 2ne^{-cd} - 2e^{-n^2 / 2}$ , we have  $|\mathcal{i}_1| \geq (1 - \eta_1 \eta_2 / 32)n$ . indeed, if this is the not true, then the set of  $i$  that does not satisfy the above inequality will exceed  $\eta_1 \eta_2 n / 32$  and thus (177) will be violated.

now, conditioning on  $\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}$ , we can view  $\sum_{\ell = 1}^{n}(b_{\ell} - \widetilde{b}_{\ell})(h_{i\ell} - \sigma (0))$  as a weighted sum of independent sub-gaussian variables  $b_{\ell} - \widetilde{b}_{\ell}$ . by hoeffding's inequality for sub-gaussian variables, we have

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} \big (\big | \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} (b _ {\ell} - \widetilde {b} _ {\ell}) (h _ {i \ell} - \sigma (0)) \big | > m ^ {- 1} t \big) \leq 2 \exp \big (- t ^ {2} / \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} (h _ {i \ell} - \sigma (0)) ^ {2} \big).
$$

we take  $t = cl(2l\sqrt{d})^b\sqrt{n}$  where  $c > 0$  is a sufficiently large constant so that the above probability upper bound (right-hand side) is smaller than  $\eta_1\eta_2 / 16$ . denote the event  $\mathcal{e}_i$  as

$$
\mathcal {e} _ {i} = \left\{\left| \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} \left(b _ {\ell} - \widetilde {b} _ {\ell}\right) \left(h _ {i \ell} - \sigma (0)\right) \right| > c m ^ {- 1} l \left(2 l \sqrt {d}\right) ^ {b} \sqrt {n} \right\}.
$$

we have proved  $\mathbb{p}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\mathcal{e}_i)\leq \eta_1\eta_2 / 32$  for  $i\in \mathcal{i}_1$ . conditioning on  $|\mathcal{i}_1|\geq (1 - \eta_1\eta_2 / 32)n$ , we obtain, by markov's inequality,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {p} _ {\tilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbf {1} \{\mathcal {e} _ {i} \} > \frac {\eta_ {2} n}{2}\right) \leq \frac {2}{\eta_ {2} n} \mathbb {e} _ {\tilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} \left[ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbf {1} \{\mathcal {e} _ {i} \} \right] = \frac {2}{\eta_ {2} n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {p} _ {\tilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} (\mathcal {e} _ {i}) \\ \leq \frac {2}{\eta_ {2} n} \sum_ {i \in \mathcal {i} _ {1}} \mathbb {p} _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} (\mathcal {e} _ {i}) + \frac {2}{\eta_ {2} n} \sum_ {i \not \in \mathcal {i} _ {1}} 1 \leq \frac {\eta_ {1}}{8}. \\ \end{array}
$$

let  $\mathcal{i}' = \mathcal{i}'(\mathbf{x},\pmb{y},\widetilde{\pmb{\theta}}) \subset [n]$  be the set of  $i$  such that the complement  $\mathcal{e}_i^c$  holds. then with probability at least  $1 - \eta_1 / 8 - 2ne^{-cd} - 2e^{-n^2 /2}$ , we have  $|\mathcal{i}'| \geq (1 - \eta_2 / 2)n$ , and for every  $i \in \mathcal{i}'$ ,

$$
t _ {i, 2} \leq \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} c m ^ {- 1} l (2 l \sqrt {d}) ^ {b} \leq c m ^ {- 1} l (2 l \sqrt {d}) ^ {b}.
$$

iii) bounding  $t_{i,3}$ . by the assumption on  $\sigma'$ , we can write

$$
\delta h _ {i \ell} = \int_ {0} ^ {1} \sigma^ {\prime} \left(t \langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \rangle + (1 - t) \langle \widetilde {\boldsymbol {w}} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \rangle\right) d t \cdot \langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell} - \widetilde {\boldsymbol {w}} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \rangle
$$

similarly as before, with probability at least  $1 - 2ne^{-cd}$ , we have  $|\langle \pmb{w}_{\ell},\pmb{x}_i\rangle |\leq \| \pmb{w}_{\ell}\| _2\cdot \| \pmb{x}_i\| _2\leq 2l\sqrt{d}$  and similarly  $|\langle \widetilde{\pmb{w}}_{\ell},\pmb{x}_{i}\rangle |\leq 2l\sqrt{d}$  for all  $\ell$  and  $i$ . this leads to

$$
\left| \delta h _ {i \ell} \right| \leq \max  _ {| z | \leq 2 l \sqrt {d}} \left| \sigma^ {\prime} (z) \right| \cdot \left| \langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell} - \widetilde {\boldsymbol {w}} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \rangle \right|.
$$

by the assumption on  $\sigma'$ , we have  $\max_{|z| \leq 2l\sqrt{d}} |\sigma'(z)| \leq b[1 + (2l\sqrt{d})^b] \leq 2b(2l\sqrt{d})^b$ . therefore, we can bound  $t_{i,3}$  as follows. for each  $i \in [n]$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {l}{\sqrt {n}} \left(\sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} | \delta h _ {i \ell} | ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \leq \frac {l}{\sqrt {n}} \cdot 2 b (2 l \sqrt {d}) ^ {b} \cdot \left(\sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} \langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell} - \tilde {\boldsymbol {w}} _ {\ell}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \rangle^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \\ \leq \frac {2 b l (2 l \sqrt {d}) ^ {b}}{\sqrt {n}} \cdot \| (\mathbf {w} - \widetilde {\mathbf {w}}) \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \| \\ \leq 4 b c m ^ {- 1} l (2 l \sqrt {d}) ^ {b} \\ \end{array}
$$

with probability at least  $1 - 2e^{-cd}$ . this is because  $\| \pmb{x}_i\| \leq 2\sqrt{d}$  holds with probability  $1 - 2e^{-cd}$ ; and also, since each entry of  $\mathbf{w} - \widetilde{\mathbf{w}}$  is independent and bounded by  $(m\sqrt{d})^{-1}$ ,

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} \left(\| (\mathbf {w} - \widetilde {\mathbf {w}}) \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \| \leq 2 c m ^ {- 1} \sqrt {n}\right) \geq 1 - 2 e ^ {- c n}, \tag {178}
$$

which is a consequence of bernstein's inequality (see [ver10] thm. 5.39 for example). taking the union bound over  $i$ , (178) holds for all  $i \in [n]$  with probability at least  $1 - 2ne^{-cd} - 2ne^{-cn}$ .

(iv) combining three bounds. finally, combining the bounds on  $t_{i,1}$ ,  $t_{i,2}$ , and  $t_{i,3}$ , we obtain that with probability at least  $1 - \eta_1 / 4 - 4n(e^{-cn} + e^{-cd}) - 2e^{-n^2 /2}$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \max  _ {i \in i ^ {\prime}} | f _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}) - f _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}) | \leq c m ^ {- 1} + c m ^ {- 1} l (2 l \sqrt {d}) ^ {b} + c m ^ {- 1} l (2 l \sqrt {d}) ^ {b} \\ \leq c m ^ {- 1} l \left(l \sqrt {d}\right) ^ {b}. \tag {179} \\ \end{array}
$$

under the asymptotic assumption  $\log n = o_d\big(\min \{n,d\}\big)$ , we have  $4n(e^{-cn} + e^{-cd}) = o_d(1)$ , so there exists a sufficient large  $d_0$  such that  $4n(e^{-cn} + e^{-cd}) + 2e^{-n^2 /2}\leq \eta_1 / 4$  if  $d > d_0$ . we choose  $m = c\delta^{-1}\cdot 2l(l\sqrt{d})^{b}$  (where the  $c$  has the same value as in eq. 179) and get

$$
\max  _ {i \in \mathcal {i} ^ {\prime}} | f _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}) - f _ {\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {i}) | \leq \delta / 2 <   \delta .
$$

with probability  $1 - \eta_1 / 2$ . this proves the claim. note that with this choice of  $m$ ,  $\log (lm + 1) = o_d\big(\log (dl / \delta)\big)$  so the lower bound is obtained.

lemma 28. the cardinality of the discrete set  $\theta_{l,m}$  defined in (172) have the bound

$$
\left| \theta_ {l, m} \right| \leq o \left(\left[ \sqrt {2 \pi e} (4 m l + 1) \right] ^ {n d + n}\right).
$$

proof. denote  $b^{d}(r) = \{\pmb{x} \in \mathbb{r}^{d} : \| \pmb{x} \| \leq r\}$ . recall the definition of the packing number. we say the set  $\mathcal{a} \in b^{d}(r)$  is an  $\varepsilon$ -packing of  $(b^{d}(r), \| \cdot \|_{\infty})$  if  $\| \pmb{x}_{1} - \pmb{x}_{2} \|_{\infty} > \varepsilon$  for every  $\pmb{x}_{1}, \pmb{x}_{2} \in \mathcal{a}$ ,  $\pmb{x}_{1} \neq \pmb{x}_{2}$ . we define the packing number

$$
d (b ^ {d} (r), \| \cdot \| _ {\infty}, \varepsilon) := \sup \left\{| \mathcal {a} |: \mathcal {a} \mathrm {i s a n} \varepsilon \text {- p a c k i n g o f} (b ^ {d} (r), \| \cdot \| _ {\infty} \right\}
$$

by the volume argument, we have the bound

$$
d \left(b ^ {d} (r), \| \cdot \| _ {\infty}, 2 \varepsilon\right) \leq \frac {\operatorname {v o l} \left(b ^ {d} \left(r + \sqrt {d} \varepsilon\right)\right)}{\varepsilon^ {d}} \stackrel {(i)} {=} o _ {d} (1) \cdot \left(\frac {2 \pi e}{d}\right) ^ {d / 2} \left(\frac {r + \sqrt {d} \varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\right) ^ {d} \tag {180}
$$

where in (i) we used the formula of the volume of a euclidean ball and stirling's approximation.

recall that  $\mathcal{d}_m = \{0, \pm \frac{1}{m}, \pm \frac{2}{m}, \ldots\}$ . to apply this result to bounding  $|\theta_{l,m}|$ , first we observe that

$$
\left| \theta_ {l, m} \right| \leq \left| \left\{\boldsymbol {a} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}: \| \boldsymbol {a} \| \leq \sqrt {n} l, a _ {\ell} \in \mathcal {d} _ {m} \forall \ell \in [ n ] \right\} \right| \tag {181}
$$

$$
\cdot \prod_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} \left| \left\{\boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {d}: \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell} \| \leq l, \sqrt {d} [ \boldsymbol {w} _ {\ell} ] _ {j} \in \mathcal {d} _ {m} \forall j \in [ d ] \right\} \right|. \tag {182}
$$

suppose  $\pmb{a}_1, \pmb{a}_2$  lie in the set on the rhs of (181) and  $\pmb{a}_1 \neq \pmb{a}_2$ . then, by the definition of  $\mathcal{d}_m$ , we must have  $\| \pmb{a}_1 - \pmb{a}_2 \|_{\infty} > \frac{1}{2m}$ . this means that the cardinality of the set on the rhs of (181) is bounded by the packing number  $d(b^n(\sqrt{n} l), \| \cdot \|_{\infty}, \frac{1}{2m})$ . by (180), we get an upper bound on the cardinality

$$
o _ {n} \left(\frac {2 \pi e}{n}\right) ^ {n / 2} \cdot \left(\frac {\sqrt {n} l + \sqrt {n} \frac {1}{4 m}}{\frac {1}{4 m}}\right) ^ {n} = o _ {n} (1) \cdot (2 \pi e) ^ {n / 2} (4 m l + 1) ^ {n}. \tag {183}
$$

similarly, we can bound the cardinality of each of the  $n$  sets in (182) by

$$
o _ {d} \left(\frac {2 \pi e}{d}\right) ^ {d / 2} \cdot \left(\frac {l + \sqrt {d} \frac {1}{4 m \sqrt {d}}}{\frac {1}{4 m \sqrt {d}}}\right) ^ {d} = o _ {d} (1) \cdot (2 \pi e) ^ {d / 2} (4 l m + 1) ^ {d}. \tag {184}
$$

recall that  $n = n(d) \to \infty$  as  $d \to \infty$ , so  $o_{n}(1)$  in (183) can be replaced by  $o_{d}(1)$ . combining (183) and (184), we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \theta_ {l, m} \right| \leq o _ {d} (1) \cdot \left[ \sqrt {2 \pi e} (4 m l + 1) \right] ^ {n} \prod_ {\ell = 1} ^ {n} \left[ \sqrt {2 \pi e} (4 m l + 1) \right] ^ {d} \\ \leq o _ {d} (1) \cdot \left[ \sqrt {2 \pi e} (4 m l + 1) \right] ^ {n d + n}. \\ \end{array}
$$
